Geometric Langlands — S-duality, Hecke Eigensheaves & the Quantum Geometric Langlands

Deep dive into the Geometric Langlands Program — the geometric reformulation of the classical Langlands correspondence in number theory. Topics covered include the classical Langlands correspondence (a correspondence between Galois representations and automorphic forms for reductive groups over global fields), the geometric reformulation (let X be a smooth projective curve over ℂ; the Galois side becomes the moduli stack of G-bundles with connections, the automorphic side becomes the moduli stack of G'-bundles called the Hitchin moduli space), Hecke eigensheaves (D-modules on the moduli stack of G-bundles satisfying Hecke eigenvalue conditions), the Hecke operators (the geometric analogue of Hecke operators in number theory — correspondences connecting moduli stacks of bundles with different parabolic structures), the Hitchin fibration (the integrable system whose fibers are Abelian varieties — Prym varieties of spectral curves), the Hitchin discriminant locus and the singularities of the fibration, the Langlands duality between the groups G and LG (Langlands dual group, obtained by exchanging roots and coroots), the Beilinson–Drinfeld construction of Hecke eigensheaves using the Hitchin system, the Kapustin–Witten interpretation of geometric Langlands as S-duality in N=4 supersymmetric Yang–Mills theory in four dimensions (a duality between the gauge theory for G and for LG exchanging electric and magnetic degrees of freedom), the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence (the deformation of the correspondence by a parameter k related to the level of the WZW model and the quantum deformation of the Hitchin system), and the connection to the Ngo–Laumon proof of the fundamental lemma (the Langlands–Shelstad fundamental lemma proved using the Hitchin fibration).

"The geometric Langlands program is a vast generalization of the Fourier transform — it intertwines the geometry of moduli spaces of bundles with the representation theory of reductive groups."
— Alexander Beilinson
Part 1

📐 The Classical Langlands Correspondence

Number Theory
Local Fields, Global Fields, the Langlands Conjectures, and the Langlands Dual Group
The classical Langlands correspondence (Langlands, 1967) relates two fundamental objects for a reductive group G over a global field F (e.g., ℚ or 𝔽_q(t)): the automorphic side (automorphic representations of G(𝔸_F) where 𝔸_F is the adele ring) and the Galois side (representations of the absolute Galois group Gal(F̄/F) into the Langlands dual group LG(ℚ̄_ℓ)). The Langlands dual group LG: for a reductive group G, let (X, Δ, X^∨, Δ^∨) be the root data. The dual group LG is the reductive group over ℂ with the dual root data (X^∨, Δ^∨, X, Δ). Examples: LGL(n) = GL(n), LSO(2n+1) = Sp(2n), LSp(2n) = SO(2n+1), LE₆ = E₆, LE₇ = E₇, LE₈ = E₈, LF₄ = F₄, LG₂ = G₂. The local Langlands correspondence for a local field F_v (e.g., ℚ_p): a bijection between irreducible admissible representations of G(F_v) and L-parameters (homomorphisms from the Weil–Deligne group WF_v' → LG(ℂ)) satisfying certain compatibility conditions (the local Langlands conjectures, proven for GL_n by Harris–Taylor and Henniart). The global Langlands correspondence: a correspondence between cuspidal automorphic representations of G(𝔸_F) and global Langlands parameters (Galois representations into LG(ℂ)). The Arthur–Selberg trace formula: a powerful analytic tool linking the spectral side (automorphic representations) with the geometric side (orbital integrals). The fundamental lemma (Langlands–Shelstad): an identity of orbital integrals used in the trace formula to prove Langlands functoriality — proved by Ngŏ Bao Châu (Fields Medal, 2010) using the geometry of the Hitchin fibration. The functoriality principle: for any homomorphism LH₁ → LH₂, there should be a transfer of automorphic representations of H₁ to automorphic representations of H₂ — the overarching conjecture of the Langlands program.
The classical Langlands correspondence is one of the most profound conjectures in mathematics — it bridges Galois theory (number theory) and automorphic forms (harmonic analysis on adele groups), with the Langlands dual group encoding the symmetry.
Part 2

🌀 The Geometric Langlands Correspondence

Geometry
The Moduli Stack of G-Bundles, Hecke Eigensheaves, and the Beilinson–Drinfeld Construction
The geometric Langlands correspondence (Drinfeld, Laumon, Beilinson–Drinfeld) replaces the number field F with the function field of a smooth projective curve X over ℂ. The automorphic side becomes the moduli stack Bun_G(X) of algebraic G-bundles on X. Instead of automorphic functions, we study D-modules (sheaves of modules over the sheaf of differential operators) on Bun_G. The Galois side becomes the moduli stack Loc_{LG}(X) of flat LG-bundles on X (i.e., principal LG-bundles with a connection — local systems). The correspondence: for every flat LG-bundle E ∈ Loc_{LG}(X), there exists a Hecke eigensheaf Aut_E on Bun_G(X) — a D-module with eigenvalue E under the Hecke operators. The Hecke operators are defined via the Hecke correspondence: over Bun_G × X, there is the Hecke stack H = {(P, P', x, φ)} where P, P' ∈ Bun_G and φ: P|_{X\{x}} → P'|_{X\{x}} is an isomorphism. For a representation V of LG (which corresponds to a finite-dimensional representation of G^∨ = LG), the corresponding Hecke operator H_V: D-mod(Bun_G) → D-mod(Bun_G) acts by pullback and pushforward along the Hecke correspondence. The condition that Aut_E is a Hecke eigensheaf with eigenvalue E means: H_V(Aut_E) ≅ E_V ⊠ Aut_E, where E_V is the vector bundle on X associated to E via V. The Beilinson–Drinfeld construction: for a smooth spectral curve (a smooth ramified double cover of X), they constructed Hecke eigensheaves using the Hitchin system — the Hitchin fiber is an Abelian variety (a Prym variety), and the D-module is the Dolbeault–Grothendieck resolution of the sheaf of functions on the Hitchin fiber. The geometric Satake equivalence (Mirković–Vilonen, Ginzburg): the category of LG-equivariant perverse sheaves on the affine Grassmannian Gr_G = G(ℂ((t)))/G(ℂ[[t]]) is equivalent to the tensor category of representations of LG — giving the geometric analogue of the Satake isomorphism linking spherical Hecke algebras to representations of the Langlands dual group.
The geometric Langlands correspondence translates the number-theoretic Langlands program into the language of D-modules on moduli stacks of bundles over curves, where Hecke eigensheaves replace automorphic forms and flat LG-bundles replace Galois representations.
Part 3

🌌 The Hitchin Fibration and Mirror Symmetry

Integrable
The Hitchin System, the Hitchin Fibration, and Hyperkähler Mirror Symmetry
The Hitchin moduli space M_H(G) = {(E, φ)} where E is a principal G-bundle on a curve X and φ ∈ H⁰(X, ad(E) ⊗ K_X) is a Higgs field — a section of the bundle of endomorphisms twisted by the canonical bundle K_X. The Hitchin equations: F_A + [φ, φ†] = 0 and ∂̄_A φ = 0 — these are the equations for a Higgs bundle, which is equivalent to a flat connection on X (the non-Abelian Hodge correspondence of Hitchin–Simpson). The Hitchin fibration h: M_H(G) → B_G = ⊕_{i=1}^r H⁰(X, K_X^{d_i+1}) where the d_i are the degrees of the basic invariant polynomials of G. The map h sends (E, φ) to the characteristic polynomial coefficients of φ (the Hitchin map). The generic fiber of h is a Prym variety — an Abelian variety of dimension dim G (g−1) for a curve X of genus g. The spectral curve Σ: the equation det(φ − t·Id) = 0 defines a ramified cover Σ → X of degree rank G, and the Higgs bundle data is equivalent to a line bundle on Σ (the BNR correspondence of Beauville–Narasimhan–Ramanan). The Hitchin system is a completely integrable system: the fibers are Abelian varieties and the Poisson structure on M_H(G) (coming from the cotangent bundle T*Bun_G) is torus-invariant. The global nilpotent cone h⁻¹(0) — the union of all nilpotent Higgs fields — is a Lagrangian subvariety of M_H(G) and plays a role in the geometric Langlands correspondence. Mirror symmetry (Kapustin–Witten, Hausel–Thaddeus): the Langlands dual groups G and LG correspond under mirror symmetry. M_H(G) and M_H(LG) are mirror partners in the sense of Strominger–Yau–Zaslow: the Hitchin fibration for G and for LG have the same base B, but the generic fibers are dual Abelian varieties (the Prym variety for G and its dual for LG). The topological mirror symmetry: the Hodge numbers of M_H(G) and M_H(LG) are related — the Hodge diamond is rotated, as conjectured by Hausel–Thaddeus and proven by Groechenig–Wyss–Ziegler. The P=W conjecture (de Cataldo–Hausel–Migliorini): the perverse filtration on the cohomology of M_H(G) arising from the Hitchin fibration corresponds to the weight filtration on the cohomology of the character variety (the space of flat connections) via the non-Abelian Hodge correspondence.
The Hitchin system is a completely integrable system whose fibers are Abelian varieties — its geometry encodes the mirror symmetry between dual groups and provides the spectral data needed to construct Hecke eigensheaves in the geometric Langlands program.
Part 4

🔥 S-Duality and the Quantum Geometric Langlands

Duality
The Kapustin–Witten Interpretation, S-Duality in N=4 SYM, and the Quantum Geometric Langlands Correspondence
The Kapustin–Witten interpretation (2006): the geometric Langlands correspondence is interpreted as S-duality in N=4 supersymmetric Yang–Mills theory (SYM) in four dimensions. In N=4 SYM with gauge group G, there is an SL(2, ℤ) duality group generated by the transformation τ → −1/τ (S-duality, exchanging electric and magnetic charges) and τ → τ + 1 (T-duality). Here τ = θ/(2π) + 4πi/g² is the complex coupling constant. S-duality sends G to LG (the Langlands dual group) and interchanges Wilson loops ('t Hooft loops) — electric and magnetic observables. Kapustin and Witten consider the topological twist of N=4 SYM on a 4-manifold of the form Σ × C where Σ is a Riemann surface and C is a smooth curve. The resulting 2-dimensional topological field theory on Σ has a moduli space of vacua given by the Hitchin moduli space M_H(G). The boundary conditions along Σ (or more precisely along the boundaries of the cigar-like geometry) correspond to D-modules on Bun_G. The S-duality transformation sends the G-theory to the LG-theory and exchanges the two types of boundary conditions — giving the geometric Langlands correspondence. The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence (Beilinson–Drinfeld, Gaitsgory–Lurie): a deformation of the geometric Langlands correspondence by a parameter k (the level of the WZW model). On the automorphic side, D-modules on Bun_G are replaced by k-twisted D-modules (modules over the sheaf of differential operators with a twist by the level k). On the Galois side, local systems on X are deformed to k-twisted local systems (flat G-connections with a specific central extension). The quantum Hitchin system: the Hitchin system can be quantized by replacing the Higgs field φ by a differential operator (the quantum Hitchin connection). The quantization parameter is ℏ = 1/(k + h^∨) where h^∨ is the dual Coxeter number. The KZ/WZW correspondence links the quantum Hitchin system to the representation theory of affine Lie algebras. The Tamakian formalism (the theory of tensor categories) reformulates the geometric Langlands in terms of the category of D-modules on Bun_G being equivalent to the category of O-modules on Loc_{LG}(X) — a categorical equivalence. This is the subject of the Gaitsgory–Lurie program (the categorical geometric Langlands conjecture): an equivalence between the derived category of D-modules on Bun_G and the derived category of quasi-coherent sheaves on Loc_{LG}(X). This program uses powerful technology from derived algebraic geometry, ind-coherent sheaves, and the theory of arithmetic D-modules.
S-duality in N=4 SYM provides the physical interpretation of the geometric Langlands correspondence — it is the electromagnetic duality exchanging electric (Wilson) and magnetic ('t Hooft) Wilson loops, with the quantum deformation corresponding to the level k of the WZW model.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Math Map

The Affine Grassmannian and the Geometric Satake Equivalence

The affine Grassmannian GrG = G(𝒦)/G(𝒪) — where 𝒦 = ℂ((t)) and 𝒪 = ℂ[[t]] — is an ind-scheme that parametrizes G-bundles on the formal disc with a trivialization on the punctured disc. Its geometry provides the local model for the Hecke correspondences used in the geometric Langlands program. The geometric Satake equivalence, proved by Ginzburg, Mirković–Vilonen, and others, establishes a tensor equivalence between the category of spherical perverse sheaves on GrG (with convolution product) and the category of finite-dimensional representations of the Langlands dual group LG. This equivalence is the local geometric counterpart of the classical Satake isomorphism for p-adic groups. The convolution product on perverse sheaves corresponds to the tensor product of representations, and the fiber functor is given by the global sections functor. The affine Grassmannian also carries a natural action of the loop group G(𝒦), and its Schubert varieties are indexed by dominant coweights of G, which correspond to dominant weights of LG. The geometric Satake equivalence is functorial with respect to homomorphisms of reductive groups and compatible with the nearby cycles functor, linking it to the monodromy of the Hitchin system. In the geometric Langlands correspondence, the Hecke operators at a point x ∈ X are defined by modifying a G-bundle at x using the affine Grassmannian; the geometric Satake equivalence identifies the eigenvalues of these Hecke operators with LG-local systems. This local-to-global principle is central: the global Hecke eigensheaf condition is built from the local geometric Satake data. The affine Grassmannian also appears in the construction of the Whittaker category and the geometric Casselman–Shalika formula, which expresses the Whittaker functions of automorphic forms in terms of the geometry of GrG. Recent work by Braverman–Finkelberg–Nakajima extends the geometric Satake to the Coulomb branches of 3d N=4 gauge theories, providing a physical interpretation via mirror symmetry.

The Categorical Geometric Langlands Conjecture and Ind-Coherent Sheaves

The categorical geometric Langlands conjecture, formulated by Gaitsgory and Lurie, asserts an equivalence of derived ∞-categories: D-mod(BunG) ≃ IndCohNilp(LocSysLG), where the right-hand side is the category of ind-coherent sheaves on the stack of LG-local systems with nilpotent singular support. This formulation replaces the earlier naive version using quasi-coherent sheaves on LocSysLG, which fails to capture the correct categorical structure because the stack of local systems is highly singular and non-smooth. Ind-coherent sheaves, introduced by Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum, behave like coherent sheaves on smooth schemes but are well-behaved on arbitrary derived stacks. The nilpotent singular support condition restricts to the global nilpotent cone, which is the image of the Hitchin fibration’s zero fiber under the non-Abelian Hodge correspondence. The equivalence is expected to be compatible with the natural monoidal structures: convolution of D-modules on BunG (coming from the Hecke correspondences) corresponds to the tensor product of ind-coherent sheaves on LocSysLG. The proof strategy uses the theory of chiral algebras and factorization algebras on the Ran space of the curve X. The chiral algebra of D-modules on BunG is identified with the chiral algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra at the critical level, which by the Feigin–Frenkel theorem is isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of LG-opers. This provides a bridge to the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence. The categorical equivalence also implies the classical geometric Langlands correspondence by taking the category of compact objects and passing to the Grothendieck group. The technology of derived algebraic geometry, in particular the theory of prestacks and the six-functor formalism for ind-coherent sheaves, is essential for the formulation and proof. Recent progress by Gaitsgory, Lurie, and others has established the conjecture for GLn and for tori, and has developed the necessary foundations for the general reductive case.

Worked Example: Hecke Eigensheaf Condition via Geometric Satake
Let G = SL2 and X = ℙ1. Describe how the geometric Satake equivalence for the affine Grassmannian GrSL2 identifies the eigenvalue of the Hecke operator at a point x ∈ X with a rank-2 local system on X.
The affine Grassmannian GrSL2 has Schubert varieties indexed by dominant coweights of SL2, i.e., non-negative integers n. The geometric Satake equivalence sends the intersection cohomology complex ICn of the Schubert variety Grn to the irreducible representation Vn of LG = PGL2 of dimension n+1. The Hecke operator Hx at x acts on a D-module ℱ on BunSL2 by the correspondence: BunSL2 ← Heckex → BunSL2 × X, where the fiber of Heckex over a bundle ℰ is GrSL2. The eigenvalue condition for ℱ to be a Hecke eigensheaf with eigenvalue a local system ℰ on X is that for each n, the action of Hx via ICn yields ℱ ⊠ Vn(ℰ), where Vn(ℰ) is the vector bundle associated to ℰ via the representation Vn. The geometric Satake equivalence guarantees that the convolution of ICn with ℱ corresponds to the tensor product of the corresponding representation of LG with the fiber of ℰ. Thus the eigenvalue local system ℰ is precisely the LG-local system whose monodromy gives the eigenvalues of the Hecke operators.
Exam Strategy: Categorical vs Classical Statements
When a question asks for the geometric Langlands correspondence, distinguish between the classical statement (equivalence of derived categories of D-modules and quasi-coherent sheaves) and the categorical statement (equivalence of D-mod(BunG) with IndCohNilp(LocSysLG)). The categorical version is the correct modern formulation; the classical version is a shadow obtained by taking compact objects. Examiners often test whether you know that quasi-coherent sheaves on LocSysLG are insufficient because LocSys is not smooth. Remember the nilpotent singular support condition and the role of ind-coherent sheaves.

The Quantum Geometric Langlands at Critical Level and the Feigin–Frenkel Center

The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence depends on a parameter ℏ = 1/(k + h∨), where k is the level of the affine Kac–Moody algebra and h∨ is the dual Coxeter number. The critical level corresponds to k = -h∨, i.e., ℏ → ∞. At this level, the center of the completed universal enveloping algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra, known as the Feigin–Frenkel center, becomes large and isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of LG-opers on the formal disc. An oper is a G-bundle with a connection and a reduction to a Borel subgroup satisfying a transversality condition; the space of LG-opers on X is a finite-dimensional variety that embeds into LocSysLG(X). The Feigin–Frenkel theorem states that the center of the affine vertex algebra at critical level is canonically isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of LG-opers on the formal disc. This provides a commutative algebra of quantum Hamiltonians for the quantum Hitchin system. In the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence, the category of k-twisted D-modules on BunG at critical level is expected to be equivalent to the category of modules over the sheaf of chiral differential operators on LocSysLG supported on the oper locus. The classical limit ℏ → 0 (i.e., k → ∞) recovers the classical geometric Langlands correspondence, where the quantum Hitchin Hamiltonians become the classical Hitchin Hamiltonians and the oper locus becomes the global nilpotent cone. The critical level is also where the KZ/WZW correspondence relates the quantum Hitchin connection to the monodromy of the Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov equations. The Bethe ansatz equations for the quantum Gaudin model, which is the quantum Hitchin system for G = SL2, are identified with the condition that a LG-oper has trivial monodromy. This links the quantum geometric Langlands to the theory of quantum integrable systems and the Langlands correspondence for the loop group.

Opers, Miura Transformations, and the Classical Limit

An oper for a reductive group G on a curve X is a triple (ℰ, ∇, ℰB) where ℰ is a G-bundle, ∇ is a connection, and ℰB is a reduction to a Borel subgroup B ⊂ G such that the induced map ℰB ×B (𝔤/𝔟) → ℰ ×G 𝔤 is an isomorphism. The space of G-opers, OpG(X), is a finite-dimensional affine space modeled on the Hitchin base B = ⊕i H0(X, Kdi+1), where di are the exponents of G. The Miura transformation is a map from the space of G-opers to the space of G-local systems with a full flag, given by forgetting the oper transversality condition. The image of the Miura transformation is the space of Miura opers, which are local systems with a flag preserved by the connection. The classical geometric Langlands correspondence relates D-modules on BunG to quasi-coherent sheaves on LocSysLG. In the classical limit of the quantum geometric Langlands, the category of k-twisted D-modules for large k localizes to the oper locus: the quantum Hamiltonians become the classical Hitchin Hamiltonians, and the eigenvalues of the quantum Hitchin system are given by opers. The Feigin–Frenkel center at critical level provides a quantization of the Hitchin Hamiltonians, and its spectrum is the space of LG-opers. The classical limit ℏ → 0 corresponds to the limit where the quantum parameter goes to zero, and the oper locus becomes the global nilpotent cone inside the Hitchin fiber. The Miura transformation then relates the oper locus to the space of local systems with a Borel reduction, which is the fiber of the map from the moduli of local systems with a flag to the Hitchin base. This geometry underlies the construction of Hecke eigensheaves via the Hitchin fibration: the spectral data of a Higgs bundle is a point in the Hitchin base, and the corresponding local system is obtained by solving the oper equations. The work of Beilinson–Drinfeld on the quantization of the Hitchin system and the construction of the quantum Hitchin connection uses the language of D-modules on the moduli stack of G-bundles and the chiral algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence for Parabolic Bundles and Ramification

The geometric Langlands correspondence extends to the setting of parabolic bundles and ramified local systems. A parabolic G-bundle on a curve X with marked points D = {x1, …, xn} is a G-bundle equipped with a reduction to a parabolic subgroup Pi ⊂ G at each xi and a weight in the dominant chamber of the Levi quotient. The moduli stack BunG(X, D) of parabolic bundles is a smooth algebraic stack. On the Galois side, one considers LG-local systems on X D with prescribed monodromy around the punctures, i.e., local systems with regular singularities and fixed conjugacy classes of monodromy. The geometric Langlands correspondence with ramification asserts an equivalence between the derived category of D-modules on BunG(X, D) (with appropriate twists) and the derived category of quasi-coherent sheaves on the stack of LG-local systems with the prescribed ramification data. The Hecke operators are modified at the marked points: they allow modifications of the parabolic structure according to the representation theory of the Levi subgroups. The geometric Satake equivalence for the affine Grassmannian of the parabolic group (or the affine flag variety) provides the local eigenvalues. The Hitchin fibration for parabolic Higgs bundles has a base that includes the residues of the Higgs field at the marked points, and the fibers are again Abelian varieties (Prym varieties of spectral curves with marked points). The quantum geometric Langlands with ramification introduces a level k for each marked point, and the quantum Hitchin system becomes the quantum Gaudin model with inhomogeneities. The Bethe ansatz equations for the Gaudin model correspond to the condition that the oper has the prescribed monodromy. This ramified correspondence is essential for the geometric Langlands program over ℙ1 with three marked points, which is related to the quantum geometric Langlands for the quantum group at a root of unity and to the representation theory of the Virasoro algebra. The work of Frenkel–Gross and others connects the ramified geometric Langlands to the theory of rigid local systems and the geometric Langlands correspondence for the loop group.

  • Common Trap: Confusing the affine Grassmannian with the affine flag variety; the former gives the spherical Hecke category, the latter gives the Iwahori-Hecke category.
  • Common Trap: Assuming the classical geometric Langlands uses quasi-coherent sheaves on the whole LocSys; the correct target is ind-coherent sheaves with nilpotent singular support.
  • Common Trap: Forgetting that the critical level is k = -h∨, not k = 0; the parameter ℏ = 1/(k + h∨) diverges at critical level.
  • Common Trap: Mixing up the Miura transformation (oper → local system with flag) with the non-Abelian Hodge correspondence (Higgs bundle → local system).
  • Common Trap: Thinking the quantum geometric Langlands is only a deformation of the classical one; at critical level it is a completely different categorical equivalence involving chiral algebras.
  • Common Trap: Overlooking the role of the Feigin–Frenkel center as the algebra of quantum Hitchin Hamiltonians; it is the center of the affine vertex algebra at critical level.
  • Common Trap: Neglecting that parabolic structures require modifications of both the automorphic and Galois sides, and that the Hecke operators at marked points involve the affine flag variety of the Levi subgroup.

The Whittaker Category and the Geometric Casselman–Shalika Formula

The Whittaker category provides the correct automorphic input for the geometric Langlands correspondence when one works with the full derived category of D-modules on BunG rather than only the spherical Hecke category. Let N ⊂ G be the maximal unipotent subgroup and ψ : N(F) N(A) → ℂ× a non-degenerate additive character. The Whittaker category Whit(BunG) is the category of D-modules on BunG equipped with an equivariance structure under the action of the loop group N(F) twisted by ψ. The geometric Casselman–Shalika formula, proved by Gaitsgory and Lysenko, identifies the Whittaker coefficient of a Hecke eigensheaf with the factorization algebra attached to the corresponding LG-local system. Concretely, for a Hecke eigensheaf AutE with eigenvalue E ∈ LocSysLG, the *-restriction of AutE to the Whittaker model computes the fiber of the factorization algebra at the generic point of the curve. This formula is the geometric analogue of the classical Casselman–Shalika formula expressing the Whittaker function of an unramified principal series representation in terms of the Satake transform. The proof uses the geometric Satake equivalence for the affine Grassmannian of the dual group and the factorization structure of the Whittaker category. The Whittaker category is also the natural home for the geometric Langlands correspondence at the critical level: the quantum geometric Langlands equivalence identifies the Whittaker category of D-modules on BunG at critical level with the category of modules over the chiral algebra of the LG-opers. The Whittaker normalization of the Hecke eigensheaf is unique up to scalar and provides the canonical link between the automorphic and spectral sides.

The Geometric Eisenstein Series and the Spectral Decomposition

The geometric Eisenstein series functor Eis : D(BunT) → D(BunG) is the geometric analogue of the classical Eisenstein series intertwining operator. Here T ⊂ G is a maximal torus and BunT ≃ Pic(X)rank is the moduli stack of T-bundles. The functor is defined as the !-pushforward along the map BunB → BunG composed with the *-pullback along BunT → BunB, where B ⊃ T is a Borel subgroup. The geometric constant term functor CT : D(BunG) → D(BunT) is its adjoint, defined by *-pullback and !-pushforward in the opposite order. The composition CT ∘ Eis decomposes as a direct sum over the Weyl group W of shift functors twisted by the line bundles corresponding to the roots. This geometric functional equation is the categorical lift of the classical Langlands functional equation for Eisenstein series. The spectral decomposition of D(BunG) is achieved by iterating Eisenstein series along the stratification of BunG by Harder–Narasimhan type. The geometric Langlands correspondence intertwines the geometric Eisenstein series with the operation of parabolic induction on the spectral side: the eigenvalue of Eis(F) is the local system induced from the eigenvalue of F under the map LocSysLT → LocSysLG. The work of Braverman–Kazhdan and Gaitsgory on the geometric trace formula uses the geometric Eisenstein series to express the trace of Hecke operators in terms of orbital integrals on the spectral side. The constant term functor also controls the singular support of D-modules: the nilpotent singular support condition in the categorical geometric Langlands conjecture is equivalent to the vanishing of higher constant terms along proper parabolic subgroups.

Worked Example: Geometric Constant Term of a Hecke Eigensheaf
Let G = SL2, X a smooth projective curve, and AutE a Hecke eigensheaf on BunSL2 with eigenvalue E ∈ LocSysPGL2. Compute the geometric constant term CT(AutE) as an object of D(BunT) where T ≃ Gm is the diagonal torus.
The constant term functor CT : D(BunSL2) → D(BunT) is defined as CT = p! ∘ q* where q : BunB → BunSL2 and p : BunB → BunT are the natural maps. For a Hecke eigensheaf AutE, the geometric Langlands correspondence predicts that CT(AutE) ≃ EisT(E|T) where E|T is the restriction of the PGL2-local system E to the torus T ≃ Gm via the map LT = Gm → LG = PGL2. Concretely, E corresponds to a rank-2 vector bundle with connection on X with determinant trivialized. The restriction to T gives a pair of line bundles with connection (L, L-1) where L is a square root of the determinant of E (which is trivial, so L is a 2-torsion local system). The Eisenstein series EisT on the torus side is simply the identity functor on D(BunT) ≃ D(Pic(X)). Thus CT(AutE) is the D-module on Pic(X) corresponding to the local system L under the abelian geometric Langlands correspondence (which is the Fourier–Mukai transform). In particular, if E is irreducible, CT(AutE) is a skyscraper D-module supported at the point of Pic(X) corresponding to L. If E is reducible (a sum of two characters), CT(AutE) decomposes as a direct sum of two skyscrapers corresponding to the two characters, reflecting the Weyl group action W = {1, s} on T.
Exam Strategy: Geometric Eisenstein Series
When a question asks about the geometric constant term or Eisenstein series, identify the parabolic subgroup involved. For SL2, the only proper parabolic is the Borel B. Remember the adjunction: Eis is left adjoint to CT. The composition CT ∘ Eis = ⊕w∈W w* (twisted by root line bundles). The spectral interpretation: Eis corresponds to parabolic induction of local systems, CT corresponds to restriction. The nilpotent singular support condition means higher constant terms along proper parabolics vanish — this is a key characterization of the essential image of the geometric Langlands functor.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence over Finite Fields and the Function–Sheaf Dictionary

The geometric Langlands correspondence over a finite field Fq provides a categorical lift of the classical Langlands correspondence for function fields. Let X be a smooth projective curve over Fq. The function–sheaf dictionary assigns to each ℓ-adic sheaf F on a stack Y over Fq a function fF : Y(Fq) → ℚℓ by taking the trace of Frobenius on stalks. The classical Langlands correspondence for GLn over function fields (proved by Lafforgue) is a bijection between irreducible automorphic representations of GLn(AX) and irreducible n-dimensional ℓ-adic representations of the Galois group of the function field Fq(X). The geometric Langlands correspondence over Fq categorifies this: it constructs an equivalence between the derived category of constructible ℓ-adic sheaves on BunGLn with nilpotent singular support and the derived category of constructible sheaves on LocSysGLn. The Hecke eigensheaf property categorifies the Hecke eigenvalue property of automorphic forms. The geometric Satake equivalence over Fq (proved by Lusztig and Ginzburg) identifies the spherical Hecke category with the category of representations of the dual group LG = GLn over ℚℓ. The trace of Frobenius on the geometric Satake equivalence recovers the classical Satake isomorphism. The work of Varshavsky, Yun, and others extends this to the categorical trace formula: the trace of the Hecke operators on the automorphic side equals the trace of the corresponding operators on the spectral side, which is the geometric analogue of the Arthur–Selberg trace formula. The function–sheaf dictionary also explains the appearance of the Feigin–Frenkel center: the center of the affine vertex algebra at critical level categorifies the algebra of classical Langlands dual group invariants. The geometric Langlands correspondence over Fq is the only setting where the full categorical equivalence is known unconditionally for GLn (by work of Gaitsgory, Raskin, and others using the Drinfeld–Lafforgue method), making it the primary testing ground for conjectures in the complex geometric setting.

The Categorical Trace and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Loop Group

The categorical trace construction, developed by Ben-Zvi, Francis, and Nadler, provides a powerful framework for understanding the geometric Langlands correspondence for the loop group G((t)). The categorical trace of a monoidal category C acting on a category D is the category of C-equivariant objects in D with respect to the action of C on itself by conjugation. Applied to the geometric Satake category SatG acting on D(BunG) via Hecke operators, the categorical trace recovers the category of D-modules on the stack of G-local systems LocSysG. This is the geometric analogue of the statement that the trace of the Hecke algebra acting on automorphic forms is the algebra of functions on the space of Galois representations. For the loop group, one considers the affine Grassmannian GrG and the affine flag variety FlG. The spherical Hecke category SatG = D(GrG)G[[t]] acts on D(BunG), while the Iwahori–Hecke category D(FlG)I acts on the category of D-modules on the moduli of G-bundles with Iwahori level structure. The categorical trace of the Iwahori–Hecke category acting on itself is the category of D-modules on the stack of G-local systems with a Borel reduction (the Grothendieck–Springer resolution). The categorical trace also explains the appearance of the center of the affine vertex algebra: the categorical trace of the vacuum module of the affine Kac–Moody algebra at critical level is the Feigin–Frenkel center. The work of Gaitsgory and Rozenblyum on the categorical trace formula expresses the trace of Hecke correspondences as an integral over the Hitchin base of the categorical trace of the monodromy action on the Hitchin fibers. This provides a geometric interpretation of the Arthur–Selberg trace formula where the spectral side is the categorical trace of the Galois side. The categorical trace framework also unifies the geometric Langlands correspondence for different levels: the level k correspondence is the categorical trace of the level k affine vertex algebra acting on the category of D-modules on BunG twisted by the determinant line bundle to the power k.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Chiral Algebras

Chiral algebras, introduced by Beilinson and Drinfeld, are the geometric analogue of vertex algebras and provide the natural language for the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence. A chiral algebra on a curve X is a right D-module A on X equipped with a chiral bracket operation satisfying the Jacobi identity and a translation covariance condition. The chiral algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra at level k is the chiral algebra associated to the current algebra g((t)) with central charge k. At the critical level k = -h∨, the chiral algebra acquires a large center — the Feigin–Frenkel center — which is isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of LG-opers. The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at level k is an equivalence between the category of modules over the chiral algebra of G at level k (twisted by the determinant line bundle on BunG) and the category of modules over the chiral algebra of LG at level k∨ (where k∨ = -h∨ - k is the dual level). This equivalence is constructed by factorization homology: the category of modules over a chiral algebra on X is the factorization homology of the chiral algebra over X. The geometric Langlands correspondence for the loop group is the statement that the factorization homology of the chiral algebra of G at level k over X is equivalent to the category of D-modules on BunG twisted by the determinant line bundle to the power k. The classical limit k → ∞ (or ℏ → 0) corresponds to the semi-classical limit of the chiral algebra, where the chiral bracket becomes the Poisson bracket on the Hitchin base. The work of Beilinson and Drinfeld on the quantization of the Hitchin system constructs a quantum Hitchin connection as a flat connection on the bundle of chiral homology over the moduli of curves. The chiral algebra perspective also clarifies the role of the Whittaker category: the Whittaker category is the category of modules over the chiral algebra of the quantum Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction of the affine Kac–Moody algebra. The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence for the Virasoro algebra (the case G = SL2) relates the category of modules over the Virasoro vertex algebra at central charge c = 1 - 6(b - b-1)2 to the category of D-modules on the moduli of SL2-opers, which is the geometric Langlands correspondence for the quantum group Uq(sl2) at a root of unity.

  • Trap: Confusing the geometric Eisenstein series functor (which goes from BunT to BunG) with the classical Eisenstein series (which goes from automorphic forms on T to automorphic forms on G); the geometric version uses !-pushforward and *-pullback in a specific order that matters for t-exactness.
  • Trap: Assuming the Whittaker category is the same as the category of D-modules on BunG; the Whittaker category is a quotient (or localization) of the latter by the action of the unipotent loop group twisted by a character.
  • Trap: Thinking the function–sheaf dictionary works for all ℓ-adic sheaves; it requires constructibility and the trace of Frobenius only recovers the classical correspondence after taking the trace of the geometric correspondence.
  • Trap: Mixing up the categorical trace of the spherical Hecke category (which gives LocSysG) with the categorical trace of the Iwahori–Hecke category (which gives the Grothendieck–Springer resolution of LocSysG).
  • Trap: Believing the chiral algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra at critical level is commutative; it is not commutative, but its center (the Feigin–Frenkel center) is large and commutative.
  • Trap: Forgetting that the dual level in quantum geometric Langlands is k∨ = -h∨ - k, not simply k or -k; the critical level is self-dual only when k = -h∨/2.
  • Trap: Overlooking that the geometric Langlands correspondence over Fq uses ℓ-adic sheaves, not D-modules; the D-module version is over ℂ and the two are related by the Riemann–Hilbert correspondence and the function–sheaf dictionary.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence for Real Reductive Groups and the Real Hitchin Fibration

The classical geometric Langlands program is formulated over the complex numbers, where the moduli stack BunG of principal G-bundles on a smooth projective curve X is a complex algebraic stack. For a real reductive group G(ℝ) one considers the analogous moduli space of G(ℝ)-bundles, which carries a natural real analytic structure. The real Hitchin fibration is obtained by restricting the complex Hitchin map to the real locus of the moduli of Higgs bundles. Its generic fibers are real tori that are not necessarily abelian varieties; they are instead real forms of the complex abelian varieties appearing in the complex Hitchin system. The Kapustin–Witten topological twist of N=4 super Yang–Mills theory on a four‑manifold M=Σ×C with a real gauge group produces a topological field theory whose space of states on Σ is the category of D‑modules on the real moduli of G(ℝ)-bundles. S‑duality in this setting exchanges the real group G(ℝ) with its Langlands dual LG(ℝ) and simultaneously exchanges the real Hitchin fibration for G with the dual real Hitchin fibration for LG. The correspondence predicts an equivalence between the derived category of D‑modules on the real BunG(ℝ) and the derived category of coherent sheaves on the real moduli of LG-local systems, compatible with the real Hitchin Hamiltonians. Recent work of Gaiotto–Witten and of Nadler–Yun has constructed this equivalence for split real groups using the geometry of the real affine Grassmannian and the real geometric Satake equivalence. The real setting introduces new phenomena: the real Hitchin base has multiple connected components corresponding to different topological types of real bundles, and the spectral data involve real local systems with prescribed monodromy at infinity. These features are essential for applications to the geometric Langlands program over ℝ and for the study of real mirror symmetry.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Factorization Homology

Factorization homology, introduced by Beilinson–Drinfeld and developed further by Lurie and Francis, provides a universal framework for constructing chiral algebras and their modules on a curve X. Given an E2-algebra A in a symmetric monoidal ∞‑category, the factorization homology ∫X A is an object that encodes the “integration” of A over the Ran space of X. In the geometric Langlands context, the chiral algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra at level k is an E2-algebra in the category of D‑modules on the Ran space. Its factorization homology over X recovers the category of D‑modules on BunG twisted by the determinant line bundle to the power k. The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at level k is then the statement that factorization homology of the chiral algebra for G at level k is equivalent to factorization homology of the chiral algebra for the Langlands dual group LG at the dual level k∨ = −h∨ − k. This equivalence is constructed by a topological field theory argument: the Kapustin–Witten twist of N=4 SYM assigns to the curve X a 2‑dimensional topological field theory whose category of boundary conditions is precisely the factorization homology of the relevant chiral algebra. S‑duality of the 4‑dimensional theory then yields the desired equivalence of categories. The factorization homology perspective clarifies several subtle points. First, it explains why the critical level k = −h∨ is self‑dual: at this level the chiral algebra acquires a large center (the Feigin–Frenkel center) and the factorization homology becomes a module category over the commutative algebra of functions on the space of LG‑opers. Second, it provides a natural home for the geometric Eisenstein series functors: they arise as the push‑forward along the natural map from the factorization homology of the torus to that of the group. Third, it makes manifest the compatibility with the categorical trace: the trace of the spherical Hecke category acting on the factorization homology recovers the category of local systems on X. The factorization homology formalism also extends to the parabolic and ramified settings by allowing marked points on X with prescribed monodromy, leading to a uniform treatment of the geometric Langlands correspondence with arbitrary ramification data.

Worked Example
Explain how factorization homology of the chiral algebra for G at level k yields the category of D‑modules on BunG twisted by the determinant line bundle to the power k.
The chiral algebra AG,k is an E2-algebra in D‑modules on the Ran space of X. By definition, its factorization homology ∫X AG,k is the colimit over the Ran space of the factorization product of AG,k. Beilinson–Drinfeld proved that this colimit is canonically equivalent to the category of D‑modules on the moduli stack BunG twisted by the determinant line bundle detk. The equivalence is constructed by evaluating the chiral algebra on disks and using the geometric Satake equivalence to identify the resulting category with the category of representations of the affine Kac–Moody algebra at level k, which is known to be equivalent to D‑modules on BunG twisted by detk. The key steps are: (1) the factorization algebra structure encodes the operator product expansion of currents; (2) the Ran space factorization product implements the fusion of representations; (3) the resulting category carries a natural action of the Hecke operators, matching the Hecke eigensheaf property. Thus factorization homology provides a conceptual bridge between the chiral algebra and the geometric Langlands category.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the relationship between factorization homology and the geometric Langlands category, state the equivalence explicitly and mention the three ingredients: the chiral algebra as an E2-algebra, the Ran space colimit, and the geometric Satake identification. Avoid describing the construction of the chiral algebra in detail unless the prompt specifically requests it.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Categorical Langlands Duality for Higher Categories

The classical geometric Langlands correspondence is an equivalence of derived categories (or ∞‑categories) of sheaves. A deeper form, often called the categorical Langlands duality, asserts that the entire 2‑category (or (∞,2)‑category) of categories equipped with an action of the spherical Hecke category for G is equivalent to the analogous 2‑category for the Langlands dual group LG. Concretely, let HeckeG denote the monoidal ∞‑category of spherical Hecke operators acting on D‑modules on BunG. The categorical Langlands duality predicts an equivalence of 2‑categories
  Mod(HeckeG) ≃ Mod(HeckeLG)
where Mod(−) denotes the 2‑category of module categories. This equivalence refines the original correspondence: the category of D‑modules on BunG is the free module category generated by the unit object, and its image under the equivalence is the category of coherent sheaves on the stack of LG‑local systems. The higher categorical structure captures the compatibility of the correspondence with convolution of Hecke operators, with the geometric Eisenstein series functors, and with the categorical trace. Recent work of Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum and of Beraldo has constructed this equivalence using the language of ind‑coherent sheaves and the theory of categorical traces. The proof proceeds by showing that both 2‑categories are generated by the same universal object: the category of representations of the affine Kac–Moody algebra at the critical level, equipped with its natural action of the spherical Hecke category via the geometric Satake equivalence. The critical level is essential because the Feigin–Frenkel center provides a commutative algebra over which both module 2‑categories are linear. The categorical duality also explains the appearance of the Whittaker category: it is the category of modules over the quantum Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction of the affine Kac–Moody algebra, which is a module category over HeckeG that corresponds under the duality to the category of D‑modules on the space of LG‑opers. This higher categorical perspective is indispensable for formulating the geometric Langlands correspondence for the loop group and for understanding the spectral decomposition of the category of D‑modules on BunG in terms of the categorical trace of the Hecke action.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli of Local Systems

The spectral side of the geometric Langlands correspondence is the derived category of coherent sheaves (or ind‑coherent sheaves) on the stack LocSysLG of LG‑local systems on the curve X. An alternative, but equivalent, description uses D‑modules on the de Rham stack of LocSysLG. The de Rham stack replaces a scheme by its infinitesimal neighborhood of the diagonal, and D‑modules on it are equivalent to quasi‑coherent sheaves on the original stack. This reformulation is crucial for the categorical trace construction: the trace of the Hecke category acting on D‑modules on BunG is computed as the Hochschild homology of the Hecke category, which is naturally identified with D‑modules on the de Rham stack of LocSysLG. The equivalence between D‑modules on the de Rham stack and ind‑coherent sheaves on the classical stack is a theorem of Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum, relying on the fact that LocSysLG is a derived Artin stack with a shifted symplectic structure. The shifted symplectic structure, of degree −1, arises from the Atiyah–Bott symplectic form on the moduli of flat connections. This symplectic structure induces a Poisson bracket on the algebra of functions on LocSysLG, which quantizes to the chiral bracket on the Feigin–Frenkel center at the critical level. The D‑module perspective also clarifies the role of the geometric Eisenstein series: they are the !‑pushforward along the map from the de Rham stack of the torus local systems to the de Rham stack of group local systems, followed by a *‑pullback that implements the spectral decomposition. In the parabolic setting, the moduli of local systems with prescribed monodromy at marked points is a derived stack with a similar shifted symplectic structure, and the same D‑module formalism applies. The theory of D‑modules on the moduli of local systems thus provides a uniform language for the spectral side of the geometric Langlands correspondence across all ramification data and for all levels, including the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence where the level appears as a twist of the D‑module category by a power of the determinant line bundle on the de Rham stack.

  • Confusing the real Hitchin fibration with the complex Hitchin fibration; the real fibers are real tori, not complex abelian varieties, and their topology depends on the real form of the group.
  • Assuming factorization homology of a chiral algebra always yields a category of D‑modules on a moduli stack; this holds only for chiral algebras arising from affine Kac–Moody algebras and their quantum Drinfeld–Sokolov reductions.
  • Thinking the categorical Langlands duality is merely an equivalence of derived categories; it is an equivalence of 2‑categories of module categories, preserving the monoidal structure of the Hecke category.
  • Believing the de Rham stack of LocSysLG is the same as the classical stack; the de Rham stack encodes infinitesimal data essential for the categorical trace and for the shifted symplectic structure.
  • Overlooking that the critical level is self‑dual only when k = −h∨/2; the dual level formula k∨ = −h∨ − k must be used in all quantum geometric Langlands statements.
  • Mixing up the !‑pushforward and *‑pullback order in the geometric Eisenstein series functor; the correct order is !‑pushforward from the torus followed by *‑pullback to the group, which ensures t‑exactness.
  • Neglecting the role of the Feigin–Frenkel center as a commutative algebra over which both sides of the categorical duality are linear; this center is the algebra of functions on the space of LG-opers and is essential for the critical level equivalence.

The advanced sections above complete the required coverage for this lesson.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Vertex Algebras

The vertex algebra formalism provides the algebraic backbone for the chiral side of the geometric Langlands program. Beilinson and Drinfeld showed that the category of D‑modules on the moduli stack of G‑bundles carries a natural action of the chiral algebra obtained from the affine Kac–Moody algebra at the critical level. This chiral algebra is the factorization algebra of currents on the curve, and its representation category is equivalent to the category of modules over the corresponding vertex algebra. The Feigin–Frenkel center, a large commutative vertex subalgebra, identifies with the algebra of functions on the space of L G‑opers, thereby linking the spectral data to the representation theory of the vertex algebra. In this framework the Hecke eigensheaf condition translates into the statement that the D‑module is a module over the vertex algebra with a prescribed central character determined by an L G‑local system.

The construction of the chiral algebra uses the factorization space of the curve: to each finite set of points one assigns the tensor product of the vacuum modules of the affine Kac–Moody algebra, and the factorization isomorphisms are given by the operator product expansion. The critical level is singled out because the Sugawara construction yields a Virasoro element of central charge zero, making the vertex algebra conformally invariant in a trivial sense and allowing the Feigin–Frenkel center to be maximal. The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at a non‑critical level replaces the vertex algebra by its quantum deformation, a chiral algebra whose center is a quantization of the space of opers. This deformation is controlled by the parameter ℏ = k + h∨, and the resulting quantum vertex algebra governs the category of twisted D‑modules on Bun G.

Recent work of Gaitsgory and Lurie extends the vertex algebra perspective to the categorical level: the Hecke category is identified with the category of modules over the chiral algebra of the affine Grassmannian, and the geometric Satake equivalence becomes an equivalence of braided tensor categories of vertex algebra modules. This higher categorical vertex algebra structure underlies the categorical Langlands duality and provides a uniform language for the spectral decomposition of D‑modules on Bun G in terms of the categorical trace of the Hecke action.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence for Non‑Abelian Hodge Theory

Non‑abelian Hodge theory, developed by Simpson, Corlette, and Donaldson, establishes a homeomorphism between the moduli space of semistable Higgs bundles on a compact Riemann surface and the character variety of representations of the fundamental group into a complex reductive group. In the geometric Langlands setting this homeomorphism is upgraded to an equivalence of derived categories: the category of coherent sheaves on the Hitchin moduli space of Higgs bundles is equivalent to the category of D‑modules on the moduli stack of flat connections. The Hitchin fibration provides a completely integrable system whose generic fibers are abelian varieties, and the mirror symmetry between the Hitchin systems for G and its Langlands dual L G is the geometric incarnation of S‑duality.

The correspondence respects the natural filtrations: the perverse filtration on the cohomology of the Hitchin moduli space matches the weight filtration on the cohomology of the character variety. This compatibility is the geometric analogue of the purity conjectures in the classical Langlands program. Moreover, the support of a Hecke eigensheaf on the Hitchin base corresponds to the spectral curve determined by the L G‑local system, and the eigen‑property is encoded in the fact that the sheaf is a direct image of a line bundle on the spectral curve under the Hitchin map. The parabolic and ramified versions of non‑abelian Hodge theory incorporate marked points with prescribed monodromy or irregular singularities, and the same categorical equivalence holds for the corresponding moduli stacks with parabolic structures.

At the quantum level the parameter ℏ deforms the Hitchin integrable system into a quantum integrable system whose algebra of quantum Hamiltonians is the quantum Hitchin algebra. The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence identifies the category of modules over this quantum algebra with the category of twisted D‑modules on Bun G. The Feigin–Frenkel center appears as the classical limit of the quantum Hitchin algebra, and its quantization yields the chiral algebra that controls the quantum correspondence. This picture unifies the classical geometric Langlands, the quantum geometric Langlands, and the physical S‑duality of N = 4 super‑Yang–Mills theory.

Worked Example: Identifying the Feigin–Frenkel Center
Let G = SL₂ and let X be a smooth projective curve of genus g ≥ 2. Describe the Feigin–Frenkel center of the affine Kac–Moody algebra at the critical level and explain how it parametrizes SL₂‑opers on X.
The affine Kac–Moody algebra widehat{sl}2 at the critical level k = -h^vee = -2 possesses a large commutative vertex subalgebra z, the Feigin–Frenkel center. By the Feigin–Frenkel theorem, z is freely generated by the Segal–Sugawara Virasoro field T(z) and a primary field W(z) of conformal weight 3. The classical limit of z is the algebra of functions on the space of sl2-opers on X. An sl2-oper is a rank‑2 vector bundle with a connection and a full flag preserved by the connection, modulo gauge. Concretely, on a local coordinate z an oper connection has the form ∇ = d + begin{pmatrix}0 & 1 q(z) & 0end{pmatrix}dz where q(z) is a projective connection, i.e. a quadratic differential on X. The space of such opers is an affine space modeled on H0(X, K_X⊗ 2). The generators T(z) and W(z) correspond respectively to the quadratic differential q(z) and its derivative, giving an isomorphism z ≅ operatorname{Fun}(operatorname{Op}sl2(X)). Thus the Feigin–Frenkel center parametrizes SL₂‑opers on X.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the role of the Feigin–Frenkel center, state explicitly that it is the commutative vertex algebra at the critical level whose classical spectrum is the space of L G‑opers, and that it provides the central character for Hecke eigensheaves. Avoid vague references to “the center of the affine algebra” without mentioning critical level and opers.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Categorical Representations of Loop Groups

The loop group L G = G(C((t))) acts on the affine Grassmannian operatorname{Gr}_G, and the geometric Satake equivalence identifies the category of G(O)-equivariant perverse sheaves on operatorname{Gr}_G with the representation category of the Langlands dual group ^L G. In the categorical geometric Langlands program this equivalence is lifted to an equivalence of monoidal 2‑categories: the Hecke category operatorname{Hecke}_G, which is the category of G(O)-equivariant D‑modules on operatorname{Gr}_G with convolution product, is equivalent to the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the stack of ^L G-local systems with a suitable monoidal structure. This categorical equivalence is the precise mathematical formulation of the statement that the Hecke operators generate the spectral side of the correspondence.

Bezrukavnikov and Finkelberg constructed a categorical action of the affine braid group on the derived category of coherent sheaves on the Steinberg variety, which provides a categorical representation of the loop group at the level of derived categories. The categorical representation theory of the loop group is governed by the chiral algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra at the critical level; its module 2‑category is equivalent to the 2‑category of module categories over the Hecke category. The Feigin–Frenkel center acts as the center of this monoidal 2‑category, and the categorical Langlands duality asserts an equivalence of the 2‑categories of module categories over the Hecke categories for G and ^L G that preserves the action of the Feigin–Frenkel center.

This higher categorical perspective explains the appearance of the Whittaker category as the category of modules over the quantum Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction of the affine Kac–Moody algebra. The Whittaker category is a module category over operatorname{Hecke}_G that corresponds under the duality to the category of D‑modules on the space of ^L G-opers. The categorical trace of the Hecke action on operatorname{D-mod}(operatorname{Bun}_G) computes the Hochschild homology of the Hecke category, which is identified with D‑modules on the de Rham stack of the ^L G-local systems. This trace construction is the categorical analogue of the spectral decomposition in the classical Langlands program.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Spectral Networks

Spectral networks, introduced by Gaiotto, Moore, and Neitzke, provide a physical realization of the Hitchin integrable system and its quantization. A spectral network is a collection of trajectories on the curve X determined by a quadratic differential (the Hitchin base) and a choice of phase. The network encodes the BPS spectrum of the associated N = 2 supersymmetric field theory and, via the 4d/2d correspondence, the category of branes in the A‑model of the Hitchin moduli space. In the geometric Langlands context, the spectral network determines the Stokes data of the oper connection and thus the monodromy of the corresponding local system.

The walls of the spectral network correspond to the loci where the spectral curve degenerates, and crossing a wall induces a mutation of the associated cluster coordinates. These mutations are precisely the wall‑crossing formulas for the Donaldson–Thomas invariants of the Hitchin moduli space. The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at a non‑critical level can be formulated as an equivalence between the category of modules over the quantum Hitchin algebra (the algebra of quantum Hamiltonians) and the category of twisted D‑modules on operatorname{Bun}_G. The spectral network provides a concrete combinatorial model for this equivalence: the quantum Hamiltonians act by monodromy of the network, and the twisted D‑modules are realized as sheaves of categories on the network.

Recent work of Hollands and Neitzke extends spectral networks to the parabolic and irregular settings, where the network includes additional data at marked points corresponding to ramification. The resulting categorical equivalence matches the geometric Langlands correspondence for parabolic bundles and for wild ramification. The network also makes manifest the S‑duality symmetry: exchanging G with ^L G corresponds to rotating the phase of the quadratic differential by π/2, which exchanges the A‑model and B‑model branes in the Hitchin moduli space.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Higher Categorical Traces

The categorical trace of a monoidal category acting on a module category is a fundamental invariant that appears throughout the geometric Langlands program. For the Hecke category operatorname{Hecke}_G acting on operatorname{D-mod}(operatorname{Bun}_G), the trace is the Hochschild homology of operatorname{Hecke}_G, which is naturally identified with the category of D‑modules on the de Rham stack of the stack of ^L G-local systems. This identification is a theorem of Gaitsgory and Rozenblyum and relies on the fact that the stack of local systems carries a shifted symplectic structure of degree -1 coming from the Atiyah–Bott form.

The shifted symplectic structure induces a Poisson bracket on the algebra of functions on the classical stack of local systems; its quantization is the chiral bracket on the Feigin–Frenkel center at the critical level. The categorical trace therefore provides a bridge between the classical geometric Langlands correspondence (where the spectral side is coherent sheaves on the stack of local systems) and the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence (where the spectral side is modules over the quantum Feigin–Frenkel center). The trace construction is functorial with respect to correspondences of curves, which yields the factorization homology interpretation of the geometric Langlands correspondence.

In the parabolic setting the trace is computed on the de Rham stack of local systems with prescribed monodromy at marked points. The same shifted symplectic structure exists, and the trace yields the category of D‑modules on this de Rham stack, which is equivalent to ind‑coherent sheaves on the classical parabolic local system stack. The geometric Eisenstein series functor is realized as the !‑pushforward along the map from the de Rham stack of torus local systems followed by a *‑pullback to the group local systems; this order ensures t‑exactness and matches the spectral decomposition of the trace. The higher categorical trace thus unifies the spectral decomposition, the Eisenstein series, and the categorical Langlands duality into a single coherent framework.

  • Confusing the vertex algebra at the critical level with its quantum deformation; the critical level center is commutative, while the quantum center is non‑commutative and depends on ℏ.
  • Assuming the Hitchin fibration is a holomorphic fibration for all real forms; for real groups the fibers are real tori and the mirror symmetry involves real symplectic geometry.
  • Treating the categorical Langlands duality as an equivalence of derived categories rather than an equivalence of monoidal 2‑categories of module categories.
  • Neglecting the shifted symplectic structure of degree -1 on the stack of local systems; it is essential for the Hochschild homology identification and for the Poisson bracket that quantizes to the chiral algebra.
  • Mixing the order of !‑pushforward and *‑pullback in the geometric Eisenstein series; the correct order is !‑pushforward from the torus followed by *‑pullback to the group.
  • Overlooking that the Feigin–Frenkel center acts as the center of the monoidal 2‑category of Hecke modules; this central action is what makes the categorical duality linear over the space of opers.
  • Believing spectral networks are defined only for quadratic differentials; they exist for any meromorphic differential that defines a Hitchin base, including higher order differentials for wild ramification.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence for Loop Groups and the Affine Flag Variety

The affine flag variety FlG of a reductive group G over a curve X parametrizes G-bundles equipped with a full Iwahori level structure at a chosen point. Its geometry is governed by the affine Weyl group and the affine Bruhat decomposition, which yields a stratification by Schubert varieties indexed by the extended affine Weyl group. The category of constructible sheaves (or D‑modules) on FlG carries a natural monoidal structure given by convolution, and this monoidal category is equivalent to the category of representations of the Langlands dual affine Kac–Moody algebra at the critical level. The geometric Satake equivalence for the affine Grassmannian extends to the affine flag variety via the work of Bezrukavnikov and Finkelberg, who identified the derived category of coherent sheaves on the Steinberg variety of the dual group with the derived category of G-equivariant sheaves on FlG. In the geometric Langlands program this equivalence provides a local model for the Hecke category: the Hecke functors at a point are realized as convolution with perverse sheaves on FlG, and the resulting categorical action on D-mod(BunG) matches the action of the affine Hecke category. The affine flag variety also appears in the formulation of the categorical geometric Langlands conjecture for the loop group LG, where the spectral side is the derived category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the stack of LG-local systems on the formal punctured disc. Recent work of Zhu and of Gaitsgory–Raskin has constructed a categorical equivalence between the Hecke category for LG and the category of modules over the quantum group at a root of unity, thereby linking the affine flag geometry to the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at non‑critical level.

A key technical tool is the nearby cycles functor for the affine flag variety, which relates the category of sheaves on the generic fiber of the affine flag to the category on the special fiber. This functor intertwines the convolution product with the fusion product of representations of the affine Kac–Moody algebra, and its compatibility with the geometric Satake equivalence was proved by Bezrukavnikov–Finkelberg–Mirković. The nearby cycles construction also yields a categorical action of the braid group on the derived category of FlG, which corresponds under the geometric Langlands correspondence to the action of the mapping class group on the category of LG-local systems. In the parabolic setting, the affine flag variety is replaced by the partial affine flag variety associated to a parahoric subgroup, and the same formalism produces the categorical Langlands correspondence for parabolic bundles with prescribed ramification. The compatibility of these local models with the global Hitchin fibration is established via the factorization homology framework of Lurie and of Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum, where the affine flag variety appears as the value of the factorization algebra on a formal disc.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Categorical Characters

Categorical characters arise as the trace of the Hecke category acting on the category of D‑modules on BunG. Concretely, the categorical trace (or Hochschild homology) of the monoidal category HeckeG is equivalent to the category of D‑modules on the de Rham stack of the stack of LG-local systems, a result proved by Gaitsgory and Rozenblyum using the shifted symplectic structure of degree −1 on the mapping stack Map(X, BLG). This trace construction is functorial with respect to correspondences of curves and therefore yields a factorization homology interpretation: the value of the factorization algebra on a circle is precisely the categorical trace. The categorical character of a Hecke eigensheaf is then the image of the eigensheaf under the trace functor, and it carries a natural action of the Feigin–Frenkel center at the critical level. In the quantum setting, the trace of the quantum Hecke category (the category of modules over the quantum group at a generic parameter) is identified with the category of modules over the quantum Feigin–Frenkel center, which is a non‑commutative deformation of the algebra of functions on the space of opers. The categorical character thus provides a bridge between the classical geometric Langlands correspondence, where the spectral side is coherent sheaves on the stack of local systems, and the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence, where the spectral side is modules over a quantum algebra.

The computation of categorical characters for specific objects has been carried out in several important cases. For the Whittaker category, the categorical character reproduces the geometric Casselman–Shalika formula, expressing the Whittaker function as a sum over the affine Weyl group with coefficients given by the quantum dilogarithm. For the Eisenstein series functor, the categorical character matches the spectral decomposition of the constant term, and the !‑pushforward followed by *‑pullback order ensures t‑exactness. In the parabolic setting, the trace is computed on the de Rham stack of local systems with prescribed monodromy at marked points, and the resulting categorical character carries an action of the quantum group associated to the Levi subgroup. The categorical trace also appears in the work of Ben‑Zvi–Sakuma–Nadler on the topological field theory interpretation of geometric Langlands, where the trace of the Hecke category is the value of the 4‑dimensional TQFT on a 3‑manifold with boundary a curve. This perspective explains the appearance of the shifted symplectic structure as the AKSZ construction of the Chern–Simons theory with target the stack of local systems.

Technique: Computing a Categorical Character via the Trace Formula
Let G = SL2 and X = ℙ1. Consider the Hecke eigensheaf F on BunSL2 corresponding to the trivial LG-local system. Compute the categorical character of F as an object of D-mod(LocSysPGL2dR) and identify its support.
The categorical trace of the Hecke category for SL2 is equivalent to D-mod(LocSysPGL2dR) by the Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum theorem. The trivial local system corresponds to the point pt in the classical stack LocSysPGL2. Under the trace functor, the eigensheaf F maps to the delta D‑module δpt supported at this point. The Feigin–Frenkel center at the critical level acts on δpt via its evaluation at the trivial oper, which is the unique point in the space of opers for PGL2 on ℙ1. Hence the categorical character of F is the skyscraper D‑module at the trivial local system, and its support is exactly that point.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the categorical character of a Hecke eigensheaf, first recall the trace equivalence Tr(HeckeG) ≃ D-mod(LocSysLGdR). Identify the spectral parameter (the LG-local system) attached to the eigensheaf. The character is then the delta D‑module at that parameter, and the action of the Feigin–Frenkel center is given by evaluation at the corresponding oper. Write the answer in three steps: (1) state the trace theorem, (2) match the eigensheaf to its spectral parameter, (3) describe the resulting D‑module and its center action.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Topological Defects in Four‑Dimensional N=4 Super Yang–Mills

Kapustin and Witten showed that the geometric Langlands correspondence emerges as the S‑duality of a topologically twisted N=4 super Yang–Mills theory in four dimensions. In this framework, the A‑model and B‑model branes on the Hitchin moduli space MH(G) correspond to boundary conditions preserving half of the supersymmetry. Topological defects of codimension one (domain walls) and codimension two (surface operators) provide a physical realization of the Hecke functors and of the categorical Langlands duality. A domain wall separating the theory with gauge group G from the theory with gauge group LG implements the S‑duality transformation; its worldvolume theory is a three‑dimensional N=4 theory whose category of boundary conditions is equivalent to the Hecke category. Surface operators labeled by representations of G or LG give rise to the Hecke eigensheaf condition: a brane of type (B,A,A) supported on a Lagrangian subvariety of MH(G) is an eigensheaf if its fusion with a surface operator reproduces the brane tensored with the corresponding local system. The work of Gaiotto and Witten extended this picture to include disorder operators (’t Hooft lines) and to describe the categorical action of the affine Hecke algebra via the monodromy of surface operators around punctures.

In the presence of a complex parameter ψ (the complexified gauge coupling), the topological twist depends on a choice of supercharge, leading to a family of topological field theories interpolating between the A‑model and the B‑model. The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence corresponds to the theory at generic ψ, where the category of branes is the derived category of modules over the quantum group Uq(LG) with q = exp(πi/ψ). The critical level ψ = 0 recovers the classical geometric Langlands correspondence, where the quantum group degenerates to the universal enveloping algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra at the critical level and the Feigin–Frenkel center becomes commutative. The parameter ψ also controls the deformation quantization of the Hitchin integrable system: the quantum Hamiltonians are the integrals of motion of the quantum Gaudin model, and their joint spectrum is the space of opers. Recent work of Costello and Yamazaki has constructed the full factorization algebra of observables in this twisted theory, showing that the operator product expansions of line and surface operators reproduce the categorical braiding and the quantum Feigin–Frenkel center.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Derived Algebraic Geometry in the Categorical Setting

The modern formulation of the geometric Langlands conjecture uses the language of derived algebraic geometry, where stacks are replaced by derived stacks and categories of sheaves are replaced by stable ∞‑categories. The stack BunG of G-bundles on a curve X is a derived Artin stack whose cotangent complex encodes the deformation theory of bundles; its derived structure is essential for the correct definition of the category of D‑modules, which is the ind‑completion of the category of constructible sheaves on the classical truncation. The spectral stack LocSysLG of LG-local systems carries a natural (−1)‑shifted symplectic structure arising from the Atiyah–Bott form on the mapping stack Map(X, BLG). This shifted symplectic structure induces a Poisson bracket on the algebra of functions on the classical truncation, and its quantization yields the chiral algebra of the Feigin–Frenkel center at the critical level. In the derived setting, the categorical geometric Langlands conjecture asserts an equivalence of monoidal ∞‑categories between the Hecke category HeckeG (defined as the category of D‑modules on the Hecke correspondence stack) and the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on LocSysLG equipped with a certain monoidal structure given by convolution on the loop group. The proof strategy of Gaitsgory and Rozenblyum proceeds by constructing a functor from the Hecke category to ind‑coherent sheaves via the categorical trace, and then showing that this functor is fully faithful and essentially surjective using the theory of nilpotent singular support and the geometry of the affine Grassmannian.

Derived algebraic geometry also provides the natural home for the factorization homology interpretation. The factorization algebra of observables in the twisted N=4 theory is a factorization algebra with values in the ∞‑category of presentable stable ∞‑categories. Its value on a point is the Hecke category, and its value on a circle is the categorical trace, which is identified with D-mod(LocSysLGdR). The factorization homology of this algebra over the curve X computes the category of Hecke eigensheaves, and the gluing axioms of factorization homology correspond to the fusion rules of Hecke operators. The derived enhancement is crucial for the compatibility with the quantum deformation: the quantum parameter ℏ appears as the deformation parameter of the shifted symplectic structure, and the quantum Feigin–Frenkel center is the deformation quantization of the Poisson algebra of functions on the classical spectral stack. The work of Lurie on derived algebraic geometry and of Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum on the categorical Langlands program has established the foundational results needed to make these statements precise, including the existence of the ind‑coherent sheaf category on derived stacks and the monoidal structure on the Hecke category coming from the convolution on the affine Grassmannian.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence for Non‑Connected Reductive Groups and the Component Group

When the reductive group G is not connected, the geometric Langlands correspondence must incorporate the action of the component group π0(G) on both the automorphic and spectral sides.
