Geometric Hecke operators are the fundamental tools on the automorphic side of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence, analogous to classical Hecke operators acting on automorphic forms. In the geometric setting, these operators act on categories of sheaves (typically D-modules or coherent sheaves) on the moduli stack of G-bundles, BunG(X). Their construction involves specific geometric correspondences, which are themselves moduli stacks.

Let X be a smooth projective curve and G a reductive group. For a point x ∈ X, a Hecke modification of a G-bundle P at x is another G-bundle P' that is isomorphic to P away from x, but differs at x in a specific way. These modifications are classified by elements of the affine Grassmannian G((t))/G[[t]]. More precisely, for each dominant weight λ of G, there is a corresponding Hecke modification. The Hecke stack Hx,λ is defined as the moduli stack of triples (P, P', φ), where P and P' are G-bundles on X, and φ is an isomorphism P|X-x ≅ P'|X-x, such that the "type" of modification at x is specified by λ. This type is measured by the relative position of the trivializations of P and P' over a formal neighborhood of x, which corresponds to an element in the double coset G[[t]]λG[[t]] in G((t)).

The Hecke stack Hx,λ comes equipped with two natural projection maps: p1 : Hx,λ → BunG(X) sending (P, P', φ) to P, and p2 : Hx,λ → BunG(X) sending (P, P', φ) to P'. A geometric Hecke operator Tx,λ acting on a sheaf F on BunG(X) is then defined as a functor:

Tx,λ(F) = (p2)* (p1)* F ⊗ Lx,λ

Here, (p1)* is the pullback functor, (p2)* is the pushforward functor (typically derived pushforward), and Lx,λ is a specific line bundle on Hx,λ, often related to the determinant of the relative position of the bundles, which ensures the correct normalization and categorical properties. These operators form an algebra under convolution, known as the geometric Hecke algebra, which is isomorphic to the affine Hecke algebra. A Hecke eigensheaf is a sheaf F such that Tx,λ(F) is isomorphic to F ⊗ Vx,λ for some vector space Vx,λ, for all x and λ. These Vx,λ are the "eigenvalues" and correspond to the evaluation of the LG-local system at the point x, providing the bridge to the spectral side of the correspondence.

Examiner's trap: Misinterpreting Hecke Operators
Consider a scenario where you are asked to describe the action of a geometric Hecke operator Tx,λ on a D-module F on BunG(X). A common misconception is to simply state that it's a "pullback and pushforward" operation. Explain why this is incomplete and what crucial elements are missing for a full understanding.
The statement "pullback and pushforward" is a partial description but misses critical components. A complete understanding requires acknowledging:
  1. The Domain and Codomain: The Hecke operator Tx,λ acts between categories of sheaves on BunG(X), specifically from D-mod(BunG(X)) to itself.
  2. The Hecke Correspondence: The operation is mediated by the Hecke stack Hx,λ, which is the moduli space of modified bundles. This stack is equipped with two projection maps, p1 and p2, to BunG(X).
  3. The Functorial Definition: The operator is defined as Tx,λ(F) = (p2)* (p1)* F ⊗ Lx,λ. The crucial missing elements in the simplified description are:
    • The specific line bundle Lx,λ: This line bundle, often related to the determinant of the relative position of the bundles or a canonical bundle, is essential for the operator to have the desired properties (e.g., associativity, compatibility with the geometric Satake equivalence). Without this twist, the functor does not correctly represent the action of the affine Hecke algebra.
    • The Derived Nature: In the modern categorical geometric Langlands, the pullbacks and pushforwards are typically derived functors (e.g., R(p2)*, L(p1)*) operating on derived categories of sheaves (e.g., Db(D-mod(BunG(X)))). This ensures the functors are well-behaved and preserve homological information.
    • The Parameter λ: The Hecke operator is parameterized by a dominant weight λ, which specifies the "type" of modification at the point x. This parameter is crucial for linking the geometric side to the representation theory of the Langlands dual group.
Therefore, a complete answer must specify the Hecke stack, the two projection maps, the twisting line bundle, the derived nature of the functors, and the parameter λ.
Mastering Complex Definitions
When encountering complex mathematical definitions like the geometric Hecke operator, break them down into their constituent parts. Identify the underlying spaces (BunG(X), Hx,λ), the maps between them (p1, p2), the type of objects being acted upon (sheaves, D-modules), and any additional twisting factors (Lx,λ). Pay attention to whether functors are derived, as this often indicates a more sophisticated categorical framework. This systematic approach helps in understanding the full scope and nuance, preventing oversimplification.

The Langlands Dual Group and its Geometric Manifestation

The concept of the Langlands dual group, LG, is central to the entire Langlands Program, serving as the bridge between the automorphic and spectral sides of the correspondence. In the geometric setting, LG plays an equally fundamental role, defining the objects on the "spectral" side of the duality.

Classically, for a reductive group G, its Langlands dual group LG is another reductive group whose root datum is dual to that of G. Specifically, the root system of LG is the dual of the coroot system of G, and vice versa. The character lattice of G becomes the cocharacter lattice of LG, and the cocharacter lattice of G becomes the character lattice of LG. This algebraic construction provides a precise way to associate a dual group to any given reductive group.

In the Geometric Langlands Program, LG manifests as the gauge group for the local systems on the spectral side. The correspondence posits an equivalence between a category of sheaves (e.g., D-modules) on BunG(X) and a category of sheaves (e.g., quasi-coherent sheaves) on LocLG(X). The objects on LocLG(X) are LG-local systems, which are essentially flat connections for LG-bundles, or representations of the fundamental group π1(X) into LG. Thus, LG dictates the structure of the "spectral parameters" that classify the automorphic objects.

The duality between G and LG is not merely formal; it is deeply embedded in the physical interpretations of the correspondence. In the Kapustin-Witten formulation, the N=4 super Yang-Mills theory with gauge group G on a Riemann surface X is related by S-duality to a dual theory with gauge group LG. Here, G is the gauge group of the electric theory, and LG is the gauge group of the magnetic dual theory. This physical S-duality provides a compelling motivation for the existence of LG and its role in the geometric correspondence.

Furthermore, the center of LG, denoted Z(LG), is closely related to the Picard group of BunG(X). Specifically, the connected components of LocLG(X) are parameterized by the group of characters of Z(LG). This connection highlights how the algebraic structure of LG directly influences the topological and geometric properties of the moduli spaces involved in the duality. The Langlands dual group is not just a theoretical construct; it is the essential ingredient that defines the "other side" of the mirror in the Geometric Langlands Program, enabling the translation between seemingly disparate geometric and representation-theoretic worlds.

Geometric Langlands and Conformal Field Theory (CFT) Connections

The Geometric Langlands Program has profound connections to two-dimensional Conformal Field Theory (CFT), particularly through the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at critical level. These connections provide a physical intuition and a powerful framework for understanding the categorical equivalences involved.

At the heart of this link is the relationship between categories of D-modules on BunG(X) and representations of certain infinite-dimensional Lie algebras, known as affine Lie algebras or current algebras. These algebras are the fundamental building blocks of chiral algebras in 2D CFT. Specifically, the critical level quantum geometric Langlands correspondence, as developed by Feigin, Frenkel, Gaitsgory, and others, posits an equivalence between the category of D-modules on BunG(X) (possibly twisted) and the category of modules over a specific chiral algebra associated with G, often a W-algebra or the universal enveloping algebra of the affine Lie algebra ĝ at the critical level.

The Wess-Zumino-Witten (WZW) model is a prime example of a 2D CFT whose chiral algebra is directly related to affine Lie algebras. The modules of these affine Lie algebras correspond to conformal blocks in the WZW model. The geometric Langlands correspondence, in this context, can be seen as relating the space of sections of certain bundles on BunG(X) to the space of conformal blocks for a specific CFT. The Hecke operators on BunG(X) find their counterparts in the action of specific operators within the CFT framework.

On the spectral side, the LG-local systems are also related to CFT. The duality between G and LG in the geometric Langlands program often corresponds to a duality between different chiral algebras or different realizations of the same chiral algebra. For instance, the B-side (spectral side) can be identified with modules over a chiral algebra associated with LG, or a different presentation of the G-chiral algebra itself. This allows for a physical interpretation of S-duality in N=4 Super Yang-Mills theory, where the electric-magnetic duality of the gauge theory translates into the geometric Langlands correspondence for the associated categories of sheaves.

The theory of factorization algebras provides a rigorous algebraic framework to connect these ideas. Factorization algebras encode the operator product expansions (OPEs) of chiral fields in CFT, and their modules correspond to categories of sheaves on moduli spaces. The geometric Langlands correspondence, when viewed through the lens of factorization algebras, becomes an equivalence of categories of modules over dual factorization algebras. This deep connection not only provides powerful computational tools from CFT to study geometric Langlands but also offers new geometric insights into the structure of conformal field theories.

The Fourier-Mukai Transform as the Geometric Langlands Functor

A central conjecture in the Geometric Langlands Program is that the correspondence is realized by a specific Fourier-Mukai transform. This provides a concrete, functorial mechanism for the equivalence between the automorphic and spectral categories. A Fourier-Mukai transform is a type of integral transform that maps objects (typically sheaves or complexes of sheaves) from the derived category of one algebraic variety to the derived category of another, using an integral kernel on their product.

For the Geometric Langlands Correspondence, the conjecture states that there exists a complex of sheaves, often denoted K, on the product stack BunG(X) × LocLG(X). This complex K serves as the kernel of the Fourier-Mukai transform. The functor FK : Db(D-mod(BunG(X))) → Db(QCoh(LocLG(X))) would then be defined for a D-module F on BunG(X) as:

FK(F) = R(pr2)* (L(pr1)* F ⊗L K)

Here, pr1 and pr2 are the projection maps from the product stack to BunG(X) and LocLG(X) respectively. R(pr2)* denotes the derived pushforward, L(pr1)* the derived pullback, and ⊗L the derived tensor product. The goal is for this functor FK to be an equivalence of categories, meaning it is fully faithful and essentially surjective.

For the simplest case, G = GL(1) (the multiplicative group), BunGL(1)(X) is the Picard stack Pic(X) of line bundles, and LocGL(1)(X) is also Pic(X) (or its dual, Pic0(X)). In this abelian case, the Fourier-Mukai transform is precisely the classical Fourier transform on abelian varieties, which is known to be an equivalence. The kernel K is typically the Poincaré bundle on Pic(X) × Pic0(X).

For general reductive groups G, the construction of such a kernel K and the proof that it induces an equivalence is a formidable challenge. The stacks BunG(X) and LocLG(X) are infinite-dimensional and highly singular, making standard Fourier-Mukai techniques difficult to apply directly. Current approaches involve working with derived categories of ind-coherent sheaves or using techniques from derived algebraic geometry. Gaitsgory and his collaborators have made significant progress in constructing parts of this functor, particularly for the critical level quantum geometric Langlands, relating it to the global sections of certain chiral algebras.

The existence of such a Fourier-Mukai kernel would provide the most explicit and canonical realization of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence, transforming the abstract duality into a concrete functorial map between categories of geometric objects.

Common Traps

  • Confusing Moduli Spaces with Moduli Stacks: Assuming BunG(X) or LocLG(X) are ordinary algebraic varieties. They are stacks, which are essential for handling automorphisms and for the categorical formulation of the correspondence.
  • Oversimplifying Hecke Operators: Believing a geometric Hecke operator is just a simple pullback-pushforward. It crucially involves a specific Hecke correspondence (moduli of modified bundles), a twisting line bundle (Lx,λ), and often derived functors, which are essential for its categorical properties and relation to the affine Hecke algebra.
  • Neglecting the Role of the Langlands Dual Group: Viewing LG as merely a formal algebraic construct. In Geometric Langlands, LG is the actual gauge group for the local systems on the spectral side, defining the geometric objects of the dual theory.
  • Ignoring the Critical Level in CFT Connections: Assuming all connections to CFT are at an arbitrary level. Many deep results, especially those involving W-algebras and the Feigin-Frenkel center, are specific to the critical level of the affine Lie algebra.
  • Underestimating the Complexity of the Fourier-Mukai Kernel: Thinking the Fourier-Mukai kernel for general G is as straightforward as for GL(1). For non-abelian groups, the kernel is a highly complex object, often a complex of ind-coherent sheaves on derived stacks, and its full construction and proof of equivalence is a major open problem.
  • Misinterpreting the "Geometric" Aspect: Focusing solely on the algebraic or representation-theoretic aspects without appreciating the fundamental role of the underlying algebraic geometry of the curve X and the moduli stacks BunG(X) and LocLG(X).
  • Assuming Smoothness Everywhere: While many arguments proceed with smooth objects, the moduli stacks involved can have singularities, and working with derived categories is often necessary to handle these complexities rigorously.

Homological Mirror Symmetry as a Framework for Geometric Langlands Duality

The Geometric Langlands Program, particularly in its physical interpretation, finds a profound connection with Homological Mirror Symmetry (HMS). While the Hitchin fibration provides a classical link to mirror symmetry, HMS offers a categorical and quantum-level equivalence. Originally proposed by Maxim Kontsevich, HMS posits an equivalence between the derived category of coherent sheaves (Db(Coh(Y))) on a complex manifold Y and the Fukaya category (Fuk(X)) of Lagrangian submanifolds on its mirror symplectic manifold X. This duality, often referred to as an A-model/B-model correspondence in string theory, provides a powerful lens through which to view the geometric Langlands correspondence.

In the context of N=4 supersymmetric Yang-Mills theory on a Riemann surface X, Kapustin and Witten demonstrated that S-duality, a strong-weak coupling duality, can be interpreted as an instance of HMS. The moduli space of Higgs bundles, denoted MH, is a hyperkähler manifold, meaning it possesses three distinct complex structures (I, J, K) that are compatible with a single Riemannian metric. This allows MH to be viewed as a complex manifold in three different ways, or as a symplectic manifold in three different ways.

The automorphic side of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence, involving D-modules on the moduli stack of G-bundles (BunG(X)), can be related to the A-model on the Hitchin moduli space MH. Specifically, objects on the automorphic side are conjectured to correspond to certain A-branes (Lagrangian submanifolds with flat connections) in the Fukaya category of MH, equipped with one of its symplectic structures. Conversely, the spectral side, involving coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of LG-local systems (LocLG(X)), is related to the B-model on the Langlands dual Hitchin moduli space MHL. Objects on the spectral side correspond to B-branes (complex submanifolds with holomorphic vector bundles) in the derived category of coherent sheaves on MHL, equipped with one of its complex structures.

The S-duality of the N=4 SYM theory then acts as the mirror map, transforming A-branes on MH to B-branes on MHL, thereby establishing the equivalence between the automorphic and spectral categories. This interpretation provides a deep physical justification for the categorical nature of the Geometric Langlands Program. Mathematically, realizing this equivalence involves constructing precise categories of D-modules and coherent sheaves that capture the A-brane and B-brane structures, often requiring techniques from derived algebraic geometry and the theory of matrix factorizations to handle the singularities and infinite-dimensional nature of the moduli spaces involved. The work of Gaiotto, Moore, and Neitzke on spectral networks further illuminates this connection, providing a bridge between the BPS states of the physical theory and the geometric structures on the Hitchin moduli space, offering a concrete path towards understanding the underlying HMS. This framework suggests that the Geometric Langlands Correspondence is not merely an analogy but a manifestation of a deeper, universal duality principle in geometry and physics.

Factorization Algebras and Chiral Algebras in the Quantum Correspondence

The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence, particularly at the critical level, finds its most profound algebraic realization through the theory of factorization algebras and chiral algebras. These sophisticated algebraic structures, originating from conformal field theory (CFT), provide a rigorous framework for understanding the "quantum" aspects of the duality, where classical geometric objects are replaced by their quantum counterparts, such as modules over vertex operator algebras.

A factorization algebra on a curve X is, in essence, a sheaf of algebras that encodes local-to-global information, allowing algebraic operations to be defined on configurations of points on X. A chiral algebra is a specific type of factorization algebra, often viewed as a sheaf of vertex operator algebras (VOAs). VOAs are infinite-dimensional algebraic structures that axiomatically capture the properties of operator product expansions (OPEs) of chiral fields in 2D CFTs. They are central to the study of integrable systems and representation theory.

In the context of quantum geometric Langlands, the automorphic side is often recast in terms of modules over a specific chiral algebra. For a given reductive group G, one considers the affine Lie algebra ĝ associated with its Lie algebra g. At a particular central charge, known as the critical level (k = -h∨, where h∨ is the dual Coxeter number), the representation theory of ĝ undergoes a significant simplification. At this critical level, the universal enveloping algebra of ĝ possesses a large center, known as the Feigin-Frenkel center (or W-algebra). This W-algebra, denoted Wk(g), is a commutative algebra that plays a role analogous to the ring of functions on the moduli space of LG-local systems on the spectral side.

The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at the critical level then posits an equivalence between the category of modules over this Feigin-Frenkel center (or, more precisely, categories of D-modules on the space of opers, which are closely related) and the category of certain D-modules on the moduli stack of G-bundles. The global sections of the W-algebra on the curve X are identified with the space of conformal blocks of the corresponding CFT. These conformal blocks, which are solutions to differential equations arising from the OPEs, are then conjectured to correspond to the Hecke eigensheaves on the spectral side of the correspondence.

The construction of W-algebras from affine Lie algebras involves a process called quantum Hamiltonian reduction. This procedure effectively "gauges" part of the affine Lie algebra symmetry, leading to a smaller, non-abelian algebra with a rich representation theory. The modules over these W-algebras provide the quantum analogues of the classical automorphic forms. This framework highlights how deep connections between algebraic geometry, representation theory, and quantum field theory converge to provide a comprehensive understanding of the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence, moving beyond classical geometric objects to the more intricate world of vertex algebras and their modules.

Examiner's technique: Connecting physical and mathematical interpretations
The Kapustin-Witten interpretation of S-duality in N=4 Super Yang-Mills theory on a Riemann surface X provides a physical framework for the Geometric Langlands Correspondence. Which mathematical concept does this interpretation primarily relate to, and how does it bridge the automorphic and spectral sides of the correspondence?

Concept: The Kapustin-Witten interpretation primarily relates to Homological Mirror Symmetry (HMS).

Bridging the Correspondence:

  1. S-duality as Mirror Symmetry: In N=4 SYM, S-duality is a strong-weak coupling duality that exchanges electric and magnetic charges. Kapustin and Witten showed that this physical S-duality can be mathematically understood as an instance of Homological Mirror Symmetry between the moduli space of Higgs bundles (MH) and its Langlands dual (MHL).
  2. A-branes and B-branes: HMS posits an equivalence between the Fukaya category of A-branes (objects related to symplectic geometry, like Lagrangian submanifolds) on one manifold and the derived category of coherent sheaves of B-branes (objects related to complex geometry, like complex submanifolds with vector bundles) on its mirror manifold.
  3. Automorphic Side (A-model): The automorphic side of Geometric Langlands, involving D-modules on the moduli stack of G-bundles (BunG(X)), is associated with A-branes on the Hitchin moduli space MH. These A-branes are typically Lagrangian submanifolds within MH.
  4. Spectral Side (B-model): The spectral side, involving coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of LG-local systems (LocLG(X)), is associated with B-branes on the Langlands dual Hitchin moduli space MHL. These B-branes are typically complex submanifolds with coherent sheaves on them within MHL.
  5. The Equivalence: The S-duality transformation in N=4 SYM physically exchanges these A-model and B-model descriptions. Mathematically, this translates to the HMS equivalence, which maps the category of A-branes on MH to the category of B-branes on MHL. This categorical equivalence precisely mirrors the conjectured equivalence between the automorphic and spectral categories in the Geometric Langlands Correspondence, thereby providing a physical realization of the duality.
Mastering Interdisciplinary Connections
The Geometric Langlands Program is highly interdisciplinary, drawing heavily from algebraic geometry, representation theory, quantum field theory, and string theory. When encountering concepts like Homological Mirror Symmetry or Factorization Algebras, focus on understanding the core idea and how it bridges different mathematical or physical domains. Instead of memorizing every technical detail, grasp the 'why' and 'how' of these connections. For instance, understand that HMS provides a categorical framework for duality, and factorization algebras provide an algebraic framework for quantum field theory structures that encode the quantum Langlands correspondence. This approach helps in answering conceptual questions that test your understanding of the program's overarching structure rather than just isolated facts.

The Geometric Langlands Program for Higher Dimensions and Topological Field Theories

While the foundational Geometric Langlands Program (GLP) is primarily formulated for algebraic curves (one-dimensional varieties), a significant frontier in modern research involves extending its principles to higher-dimensional varieties, such as surfaces (two-dimensional) and three-manifolds. This extension is far from straightforward, as the geometric objects and categorical structures become considerably more complex. The physical intuition for these higher-dimensional analogues often comes from higher-dimensional topological field theories (TFTs).

For surfaces, the direct generalization of the moduli stack of G-bundles and LG-local systems faces immediate challenges. The notion of a "local system" on a surface, for instance, can be reinterpreted in various ways, sometimes involving flat connections on bundles over higher-dimensional spaces whose boundary is the surface. The geometric Hecke operators, which are central to the correspondence on curves, also require sophisticated reformulation in higher dimensions, often involving higher categorical structures or objects from higher gauge theory.

One promising avenue involves the use of 3D and 4D topological field theories. For example, the 4D N=4 Super Yang-Mills theory, which provides the physical underpinning for the GLP on curves via the Kapustin-Witten equations, can be considered on a 4-manifold. When this 4-manifold has a boundary, say a Riemann surface X, the theory on the boundary can be related to the GLP on X. Extending this, one might consider 3D N=4 theories on a 3-manifold M. If M has a boundary, say a surface Σ, then categories of boundary conditions for the 3D theory on Σ could potentially realize a higher-dimensional GLP for surfaces. The S-duality of the 3D theory would then provide the duality mechanism.

The mathematical formalization of these ideas relies heavily on advanced concepts from higher category theory, particularly (∞,n)-categories, which allow for the rigorous description of n-dimensional field theories. The objects on the automorphic side might be interpreted as "n-bundles" or objects in a higher gauge theory, while the spectral side might involve higher local systems or representations of higher fundamental groups. Connections to knot theory and link invariants also emerge in these 3D settings, where the categories of objects on the boundary of a 3-manifold can be related to invariants of knots and links embedded within the manifold.

This area of research is highly speculative and involves pushing the boundaries of both algebraic geometry and quantum topology. It aims to develop a "higher" Langlands correspondence that would unify various dualities observed in different dimensions and provide a deeper understanding of the fundamental symmetries underlying geometry and physics. The challenges include constructing the appropriate moduli spaces (which are often derived stacks in higher categories), defining the analogues of Hecke operators, and proving the categorical equivalences in these complex settings. Despite the formidable technical hurdles, the prospect of a unified Langlands program across dimensions remains a powerful driving force for research.

The Arithmetic Langlands Program and its Geometric Analogues

The Geometric Langlands Program (GLP) is not merely an abstract mathematical construction; it serves as a profound and illuminating analogue to the classical, or arithmetic, Langlands Program. The arithmetic Langlands Program, a cornerstone of modern number theory, seeks to establish deep connections between representations of Galois groups of number fields and automorphic forms on adelic groups. The GLP provides a "geometric laboratory" where many of the intricate conjectures of the arithmetic program can be reinterpreted, tested, and sometimes proven using the powerful tools of algebraic geometry and representation theory over function fields.

The core of this relationship lies in the function-sheaf dictionary, which establishes a formal analogy between number fields and function fields of algebraic curves over finite fields:

  • Number Field K corresponds to the Function Field k(X) of an algebraic curve X over a finite field k.
  • Ring of Integers OK corresponds to the Ring of Regular Functions OX on X.
  • Prime Ideals p in OK correspond to Closed Points x on X.
  • Absolute Galois Group Gal(K̄/K) corresponds to the Étale Fundamental Group π1,ét(X) of X.
  • L-functions in number theory correspond to Zeta Functions of curves over finite fields.
  • Galois Representations (representations of Gal(K̄/K)) correspond to LG-local systems (representations of π1,ét(X)).
  • Automorphic Forms on adelic groups correspond to D-modules or perverse sheaves on moduli stacks of G-bundles.

This dictionary allows mathematicians to translate conjectures from the arithmetic setting into the more tractable geometric setting. For instance, the categorical formulation of the GLP, which posits an equivalence between categories of D-modules and categories of coherent sheaves, suggests that the arithmetic Langlands correspondence should also be an equivalence of categories, a much stronger statement than the classical bijection between sets of objects.

However, the analogy is not perfect, and significant challenges remain in transferring insights directly from geometry to arithmetic. A major hurdle is the lack of a direct geometric analogue for the spectrum of integers, Spec(Z), or for general number fields. There is no "geometric curve" whose function field is a number field. Consequently, tools like the Hitchin fibration, Fourier-Mukai transforms, and derived categories, which are fundamental to the GLP, do not have immediate arithmetic counterparts.

The p-adic geometric Langlands program attempts to bridge this gap by working over p-adic fields, which share characteristics with both number fields and function fields. This area explores connections between p-adic Galois representations and p-adic automorphic forms, often drawing inspiration from the geometric insights. Despite the inherent difficulties, the geometric Langlands Program continues to be an invaluable source of intuition, guiding the formulation of new conjectures and providing a conceptual blueprint for the ultimate unification of number theory and representation theory through the arithmetic Langlands Program.

Common Traps in Advanced Geometric Langlands Concepts

  • Confusing Classical Mirror Symmetry with Homological Mirror Symmetry: While both involve duality, HMS is a much more precise categorical statement, equating derived categories of objects (A-branes and B-branes), which is crucial for its application to the categorical Geometric Langlands.
  • Oversimplifying the "Quantum" Aspect: Assuming "quantum" merely means non-commutative or involving operators. In the context of Factorization/Chiral Algebras, it refers to the specific algebraic structures (Vertex Operator Algebras, W-algebras) that encode operator product expansions and conformal blocks from 2D CFTs, particularly at the critical level.
  • Ignoring the Critical Level: Many deep results involving W-algebras and the Feigin-Frenkel center are specific to the critical level of the affine Lie algebra (k = -h∨). Assuming these results hold at arbitrary levels is a common misconception.
  • Directly Extrapolating from Curves to Higher Dimensions: Believing that the concepts and techniques for the GLP on curves can be directly applied to surfaces or higher dimensions without significant modification. Higher dimensions require new definitions for moduli spaces, Hecke operators, and often involve higher category theory and topological field theories.
  • Equating the Function-Sheaf Dictionary with a Full Analogy: While the dictionary is powerful for intuition, it does not imply a perfect one-to-one correspondence between the arithmetic and geometric programs. Significant conceptual and technical gaps remain, especially concerning the lack of a "geometric curve" for number fields.
  • Underestimating the Role of Derived Algebraic Geometry: Many advanced aspects, especially those involving HMS, higher dimensions, and the refined categorical GLP, critically rely on the framework of derived algebraic geometry to handle singularities and define categories of objects rigorously.
  • Misinterpreting the Nature of Moduli Stacks: Even at advanced levels, it's easy to forget that BunG(X) and LocLG(X) are stacks, not just varieties, and this stacky nature is fundamental for the categorical formulation and handling of automorphisms.

The geometric perspective, with its rich array of tools from algebraic geometry and topology, offers a unique vantage point for understanding the deep symmetries that govern both the arithmetic and geometric worlds.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence for Kac–Moody Groups and Affine Lie Algebras

Extending the Geometric Langlands Program beyond reductive algebraic groups to Kac–Moody groups introduces profound structural changes. For a reductive group G, the moduli stack BunG(X) of G-bundles on a smooth projective curve X is an algebraic stack of finite type. When G is replaced by an affine Kac–Moody group Ĝ (the loop group LG completed by a central extension), the moduli stack BunĜ(X) parametrizes Ĝ-bundles, which are equivalent to G-bundles on X equipped with a connection on the determinant line bundle — in other words, G-bundles with a level structure. The Langlands dual of an affine Kac–Moody group is another affine Kac–Moody group Ĝ∨ at the dual level, and the correspondence predicts an equivalence between categories of D-modules on BunĜ(X) and ind-coherent sheaves on the moduli stack LocĜ∨(X) of Ĝ∨-local systems. The critical level k = −h∨ plays a distinguished role: at this level the center of the completed universal enveloping algebra Ũ(ĝ)crit is isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of Ĝ∨-opers on the formal disc, by the Feigin–Frenkel theorem. This isomorphism underpins the construction of Hecke eigensheaves for affine Kac–Moody groups. The affine Grassmannian GrĜ for a Kac–Moody group is an ind-scheme of infinite type, and the geometric Satake equivalence relates the category of spherical representations of Ĝ to the category of perverse sheaves on GrĜ. However, the affine flag variety FlĜ and the Iwahori–Hecke algebra at critical level require the theory of categorical representations of the affine braid group and the Kazhdan–Lusztig equivalence for affine Lie algebras. A major technical obstacle is that BunĜ(X) is not of finite type, so the standard formalism of D-modules on algebraic stacks must be replaced by the theory of D-modules on ind-stacks developed by Gaitsgory and Rozenblyum. The categorical geometric Langlands conjecture for Kac–Moody groups asserts an equivalence between the category D-mod(BunĜ(X)) and the category IndCohNilp(LocĜ∨(X)) of ind-coherent sheaves with nilpotent singular support. This equivalence is expected to intertwine the action of the spherical Hecke category (constructed from the affine Grassmannian) with the action of the quantum group Uq(ĝ∨) at a root of unity on the spectral side. The quantum parameter q is related to the level by q = exp(πi/(k + h∨)). At critical level q = 1, the quantum group degenerates to the universal enveloping algebra, and the correspondence reduces to the classical geometric Langlands for the Langlands dual affine Kac–Moody group. Recent work by Braverman, Finkelberg, and Nakajima on Coulomb branches of 3d N=4 gauge theories provides a physical realization of the affine Grassmannian and the geometric Satake equivalence for Kac–Moody groups, linking the correspondence to the theory of quantum K-theory and elliptic stable envelopes.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Quantum Groups at Roots of Unity

The appearance of quantum groups at roots of unity in the Geometric Langlands Program is not merely a deformation of the classical correspondence; it reflects the monodromy of the KZ connection on conformal blocks and the braided tensor structure of the category of representations of the affine Lie algebra at non-critical level. Let ĝ be an affine Kac–Moody algebra at level k ≠ −h∨. The category KLk(ĝ) of integrable highest-weight representations of ĝ at level k is a modular tensor category, equivalent to the category of representations of the quantum group Uq(g) at q = exp(πi/(k + h∨)), where g is the finite-dimensional simple Lie algebra. In the geometric Langlands correspondence, this equivalence manifests as follows: the spherical Hecke category SphG (the category of G[[t]]-equivariant perverse sheaves on the affine Grassmannian GrG) acts on D-mod(BunG(X)) by Hecke modifications. Under the geometric Satake equivalence, SphG is identified with the category Rep(G∨) of finite-dimensional representations of the Langlands dual group. At non-critical level, the Hecke category is deformed to the quantum Hecke category, which is equivalent to the category Rep(Uq(g∨)) of representations of the quantum group at a root of unity. The parameter q is determined by the level k and the Planck constant ħ of the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence: q = exp(ħ) with ħ = πi/(k + h∨). The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at non-critical level posits an equivalence between the category of twisted D-modules on BunG(X) (twisted by the determinant line bundle raised to the level k) and the category of q-deformed coherent sheaves on LocG∨(X). The twisting on the automorphic side corresponds to the quantum parameter on the spectral side. The R-matrix of the quantum group governs the braiding of Hecke operators at distinct points, and the modular transformation of conformal blocks corresponds to the S-duality transformation exchanging the automorphic and spectral categories. A concrete realization is given by the Bezrukavnikov–Finkelberg equivalence between the category of G-equivariant perverse sheaves on the affine flag variety and the category of Uq(g)-modules at a root of unity. This equivalence is a local model for the global quantum geometric Langlands correspondence. The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at roots of unity is also intimately connected to the 3d-3d correspondence in physics: the category of boundary conditions in 3d N=2 Chern–Simons theory with gauge group G at level k is equivalent to the category of representations of Uq(g), and the geometric Langlands correspondence for the curve X arises from compactifying the 6d (2,0) theory on X × Σ where Σ is a 3-manifold. The Verlinde formula for the dimension of conformal blocks is recovered as the categorical trace of the identity functor on the quantum Hecke category.

Worked Example: Quantum Parameter from Level
Let G = SL(2) with dual Coxeter number h∨ = 2. Compute the quantum parameter q for the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at level k = 1. Then determine the order of the root of unity.
For SL(2), h∨ = 2. The quantum parameter is q = exp(πi/(k + h∨)) = exp(πi/(1 + 2)) = exp(πi/3). This is a primitive 6th root of unity since (πi/3) × 6 = 2πi. The quantum group Uq(sl2) at this q has a finite-dimensional representation category equivalent to the modular tensor category of integrable representations of the affine Lie algebra ŝl(2) at level k = 1. The order of the root of unity is 6.
Exam Strategy: Quantum Parameter Mapping
Memorize the formula q = exp(πi/(k + h∨)) and its physical interpretation: k + h∨ is the shifted level. For simply-laced groups, the order of q is 2(k + h∨). For non-simply-laced groups, the dual Coxeter number of the Langlands dual group appears. Questions often test whether you confuse the level k with the shifted level k + h∨, or whether you use the Coxeter number h instead of the dual Coxeter number h∨. Always identify the group and its dual Coxeter number first.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Elliptic Stable Envelopes and K-Theoretic Hall Algebras

A frontier of the Geometric Langlands Program lies in its K-theoretic and elliptic generalizations, where the category of D-modules is replaced by the category of coherent sheaves on derived moduli stacks, and the Hitchin fibration is replaced by the elliptic Hitchin system. The K-theoretic geometric Langlands correspondence conjectures an equivalence between the category QCoh(BunG(X)) of quasi-coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of G-bundles and the category IndCoh(LocG∨(X)) of ind-coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of G∨-local systems, compatible with the action of the K-theoretic Hecke category. The K-theoretic Hecke category is constructed from the K-theory of the affine Grassmannian and is closely related to the quantum K-theory of the flag variety. The elliptic stable envelopes of Okounkov and Aganagic provide a geometric construction of the R-matrix for the quantum group Uq(ĝ) in terms of the elliptic cohomology of the Nakajima quiver varieties. These stable envelopes are sections of a line bundle on the product of two elliptic curves associated to the quiver variety, and their wall-crossing behavior encodes the quantum dynamical Yang–Baxter equation. The K-theoretic Hall algebra (or cohomological Hall algebra) of a quiver with potential, introduced by Kontsevich and Soibelman and developed by Schiffmann, Vasserot, and others, provides an algebraic model for the spherical Hecke algebra in the K-theoretic setting. For the Jordan quiver, the K-theoretic Hall algebra is isomorphic to the elliptic Hall algebra of Burban and Schiffmann, which acts on the elliptic cohomology of the Hilbert scheme of points on an elliptic curve. This action is the K-theoretic analogue of the geometric Satake equivalence. The elliptic geometric Langlands correspondence further incorporates the modular parameter τ of the elliptic curve, leading to a two-parameter deformation (q, t) of the correspondence, where q = exp(2πiτ) and t is the equivariant parameter for the C×-action on the Hitchin base. The elliptic quantum group of Felder and the dynamical quantum group of Etingof and Varchenko appear as the symmetry algebras of the elliptic correspondence. A key result by Braverman, Finkelberg, and Nakajima identifies the Coulomb branch of a 3d N=4 gauge theory with the affine Grassmannian and shows that the quantum K-theory of the Coulomb branch is isomorphic to the spherical K-theoretic Hall algebra. This provides a physical derivation of the K-theoretic geometric Satake equivalence. The elliptic stable envelopes also compute the elliptic genus of the moduli space of G-instantons on R4, linking the correspondence to N=2 supersymmetric gauge theory and the AGT correspondence. The K-theoretic geometric Langlands correspondence for GL(n) has been established by Gaitsgory and others using the theory of factorization algebras in K-theory, but the general case for arbitrary reductive groups remains open and is the subject of intense current research.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Drinfeld Compactification and the Vinberg Monoid

The Drinfeld compactification of the moduli stack BunG(X) and the Vinberg monoid provide a geometric framework for understanding the degenerations of the geometric Langlands correspondence and the boundary behavior of Hecke eigensheaves. For a reductive group G, the Vinberg monoid MG is a normal affine algebraic monoid with group of units G × G, constructed as the spectrum of the algebra of matrix coefficients of all finite-dimensional representations of G. It admits a G × G-equivariant embedding G MG and a moment map MG → Spec(Sym(g∨)) to the commuting variety. The Drinfeld compactification BunG(X) of the moduli stack of G-bundles is defined as the moduli stack of MG-bundles on X whose generic fiber is a G-bundle. This compactification resolves the singularities of the Uhlenbeck compactification and provides a natural setting for the geometric Eisenstein series and the spectral decomposition. The boundary strata of BunG(X) are indexed by dominant coweights of G and are isomorphic to products of BunL(X) for Levi subgroups L ⊂ G. The geometric Langlands correspondence extends to the compactification: the category D-mod(BunG(X)) is equivalent to the category of ind-coherent sheaves on the Drinfeld compactification of LocG∨(X), where the latter is defined using the Vinberg monoid of the dual group MG∨. The Hecke operators extend to the compactification as correspondences between Drinfeld compactifications, and their action on the boundary strata recovers the constant term functors of the geometric Eisenstein series. The Vinberg monoid also plays a central role in the geometric Langlands correspondence for parabolic bundles: a parabolic G-bundle with parabolic structure at a divisor D ⊂ X is equivalent to an MG-bundle on X with a reduction to G on X D. The Drinfeld compactification is essential for the proof of the geometric Langlands conjecture for GL(n) by Lafforgue and for the categorical geometric Langlands conjecture by Gaitsgory and Lurie, because it allows the use of properness arguments and the decomposition theorem for the Hitchin fibration extended to the compactification. The Vinberg monoid also appears in the theory of the geometric Satake equivalence for the affine Grassmannian: the convolution product on the affine Grassmannian is defined using the Beilinson–Drinfeld Grassmannian, which is a family of affine Grassmannians over Xn compactified using the Vinberg monoid. The factorization property of the geometric Satake equivalence is a direct consequence of the monoidal structure of the Vinberg monoid. In the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence, the Vinberg monoid is replaced by the quantum Vinberg monoid, a non-commutative deformation whose representation theory is governed by the quantum group at a root of unity.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence for Supergroups and the Theory of Superconformal Blocks

The extension of the Geometric Langlands Program to supergroups — algebraic supergroups such as GL(m|n), OSp(m|2n), and the exceptional supergroups — introduces a rich interplay between bosonic and fermionic symmetries, superconformal field theory, and supergeometry. A supergroup G is a group object in the category of supermanifolds; its Lie superalgebra g = g0 ⊕ g1 decomposes into even and odd parts. The moduli stack BunG(X) of G-bundles on a curve X is a superstack, and the category of D-modules on it must be replaced by the category of D-modules on superstacks, developed by Gaitsgory and others using derived algebraic supergeometry. The Langlands dual supergroup G∨ is defined by dualizing the root datum of G; for GL(m|n) the dual is GL(n|m), and for OSp(m|2n) the dual is OSp(2n|m). The geometric Langlands correspondence for supergroups predicts an equivalence between the category of D-modules on BunG(X) and the category of ind-coherent sheaves on LocG∨(X), where the latter is the moduli stack of G∨-local systems (super-local systems). The Hecke operators are defined using the super affine Grassmannian GrG, which is an ind-superscheme. The geometric Satake equivalence for supergroups relates the category of spherical representations of the super loop group LG to the category of perverse sheaves on GrG. A crucial difference from the purely bosonic case is the presence of atypical representations — representations whose highest weight lies on the atypicality walls defined by the odd roots. These representations have vanishing superdimension and lead to non-semisimple tensor categories. The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence for supergroups involves the quantum supergroup Uq(g) at a root of unity, whose representation category is a braided tensor supercategory. The superconformal blocks of the WZW model for a supergroup at level k provide the conformal field theory realization of the correspondence. The super Virasoro algebra and the super W-algebras (such as the super Wn algebra) appear as the chiral algebras of the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at critical level. The Feigin–Frenkel center for affine Lie superalgebras is isomorphic to the algebra of superopers — superconnections with a specific gauge-fixing condition. The Miura transformation for supergroups relates superopers to super Miura opers, which are superconnections with a reduction to a Borel subalgebra. The geometric Langlands correspondence for supergroups with parabolic structures is relevant for the AGT correspondence with N=2 superconformal boundary conditions and for the 3d-3d correspondence with N=3 Chern–Simons theory. Recent work by Creutzig, Gaiotto, and others has established the geometric Langlands correspondence for GL(1|1) and OSp(1|2) using the theory of vertex operator superalgebras and factorization homology. The super Hitchin system and the super spectral curve provide the classical integrable system underlying the correspondence.

  • Confusing the shifted level k + h∨ with the bare level k when computing the quantum parameter q = exp(πi/(k + h∨)). The shifted level is the correct denominator; using k alone gives the wrong root of unity.
  • Assuming the quantum group at a root of unity is semisimple. At roots of unity, the representation category of Uq(g) is non-semisimple (except for very small ranks), and the geometric Langlands correspondence involves the full non-semisimple category, not just the semisimple quotient.
  • Overlooking the role of the Vinberg monoid in compactifying BunG. The Drinfeld compactification using the Vinberg monoid is essential for properness of the Hitchin fibration and for defining the geometric Eisenstein series; the Uhlenbeck compactification is insufficient for the categorical formulation.
  • Treating supergroups as ordinary groups with extra fermions. The representation theory of supergroups is fundamentally different: atypical representations, vanishing superdimension, and non-semisimple tensor categories require the full machinery of supergeometry and derived algebraic supergeometry.
  • Confusing the elliptic parameter τ with the quantum parameter q. In the elliptic geometric Langlands correspondence, q = exp(2πiτ) is the elliptic nome, while the quantum parameter for the quantum group is a separate deformation parameter; the two-parameter deformation (q, t) must be distinguished.
  • Assuming the K-theoretic geometric Langlands correspondence is a straightforward K-theory analogue of the D-module correspondence. The K-theoretic version involves quantum K-theory, elliptic stable envelopes, and K-theoretic Hall algebras, which have no direct D-module counterparts and require new geometric constructions.
  • Neglecting the critical level k = −h∨ as a degenerate limit. Many results (Feigin–Frenkel center, W-algebras, classical opers) are specific to the critical level; assuming they hold at arbitrary levels is a common error. The quantum parameter q = 1 at critical level, and the quantum group degenerates to the universal enveloping algebra.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Categorical Hecke Operators

The geometric Hecke operators are the categorical avatars of the classical Hecke correspondences. For a smooth projective curve X over an algebraically closed field and a reductive group G, the Hecke stack HeckeG parametrizes modifications of G-bundles at a point of X. The correspondence diagram

Hecke_G / \ / \ Bun_G Bun_G × X

induces a functor Hx from the derived category of D‑modules on BunG to itself, for each point x ∈ X. In the categorical geometric Langlands program, these functors are upgraded to an action of the monoidal category Rep(Ĝ) (or its ind‑coherent analogue) on D‑mod(BunG). The compatibility of this action with the geometric Satake equivalence is the precise formulation of the Hecke eigen‑property: a Hecke eigensheaf F satisfies HV(F) ≃ F ⊠ V for every representation V of the Langlands dual group Ĝ, where HV is the Hecke functor attached to V. Recent work of Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum and of Arinkin–Gaitsgory constructs the categorical Hecke operators using the theory of factorization algebras on the Ran space of X. This construction makes the commutativity of Hecke functors at distinct points manifest and provides a natural framework for the “spectral” action of the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on LocĜ. The categorical Hecke operators also control the geometric Eisenstein series: the constant term functor is obtained by averaging over the Hecke action, and the spectral decomposition of D‑mod(BunG) is governed by the joint eigen‑objects for the whole Hecke monoidal category.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence for Wild Ramification and Irregular Singularities

Classical geometric Langlands treats only tame ramification, where the local monodromy of a G-local system is quasi‑unipotent. Wild ramification allows arbitrary irregular singularities, encoded by a Stokes datum at each marked point. The moduli stack LocG(X, D) of G-local systems with prescribed irregular type along a divisor D is an Artin stack of infinite type, but its formal neighbourhood at a point is described by the wild character variety of Boalch. The categorical geometric Langlands conjecture with wild ramification predicts an equivalence between the category of D‑modules on the moduli stack BunG(X, D) of G-bundles with parabolic structure at D and the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on LocĜ(X, D). The Hecke operators are modified by inserting the wild ramification data: the Hecke stack now parametrizes modifications that preserve the irregular type. The work of Frenkel–Gross, of Boalch, and of Arinkin–Gaitsgory–Kazhdan–Raskin–Rozenblyum–Varshavsky establishes this equivalence for GLn using the theory of irregular conformal blocks and the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at critical level. The irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence identifies the de Rham moduli space with the Betti moduli space equipped with Stokes matrices; this identification is the analytic backbone of the wild geometric Langlands correspondence. The categorical trace of the wild Hecke category yields the wild geometric trace formula, which relates orbital integrals on the wild character variety to spectral data on the dual side.

Worked Example: Hecke Eigen‑Condition for GL(2)
Let X be a smooth projective curve, G = GL(2), and Ĝ = GL(2). Let V be the standard two‑dimensional representation of Ĝ. Suppose F is a D‑module on BunGL(2) such that the Hecke functor HV satisfies HV(F) ≃ F ⊠ V as objects in D‑mod(BunGL(2)) ⊠ Rep(GL(2)). Show that the support of F is contained in the locus of GL(2)-bundles admitting a reduction to a Borel subgroup.
The Hecke functor HV at a point x modifies a bundle E by an elementary transformation of type V. The eigen‑condition HV(F) ≃ F ⊠ V means that for any test object, the cohomology of F on the modified bundle is canonically isomorphic to the cohomology on the original bundle tensored with V. By the geometric Satake equivalence, the Hecke functor is given by convolution with the perverse sheaf ICV on the affine Grassmannian GrGL(2). The eigen‑condition forces the singular support of F to be contained in the global nilpotent cone, which for GL(2) coincides with the locus of bundles admitting a Borel reduction (the “Hecke eigensheaf” property). Concretely, if E does not admit a Borel reduction, the Hecke modification by V changes the Harder–Narasimhan type, and the convolution with ICV cannot preserve the isomorphism class of F. Hence F must be supported on the Borel‑reducible locus. This argument generalizes to higher rank: Hecke eigensheaves are supported on the “global nilpotent cone” inside BunG.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the categorical formulation of the Hecke eigen‑property, write the monoidal action of Rep(Ĝ) on D‑mod(BunG) and the isomorphism HV(F) ≃ F ⊠ V. Mention the role of the geometric Satake equivalence in identifying Hecke functors with convolution by IC‑sheaves on the affine Grassmannian. For wild ramification, recall that the moduli of local systems is replaced by the wild character variety and the Hecke stack is modified by Stokes data. These two points cover the majority of advanced exam items on categorical Hecke operators.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Categorical Centers and Drinfeld Centers

The Drinfeld center of a monoidal category C, denoted Z(C), consists of pairs (X, γ) where X ∈ C and γ is a half‑braiding with every object of C. In geometric Langlands, the category D‑mod(BunG) carries a natural monoidal structure given by convolution on the affine Grassmannian (via the geometric Satake equivalence). Its Drinfeld center is equivalent to the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the derived stack of Ĝ-local systems, IndCoh(LocĜ). This equivalence is a categorical incarnation of the statement that Hecke eigensheaves are precisely the objects of the center. The categorical center of Rep(Ĝ) (the category of representations of the Langlands dual group) is the category of Ĝ-equivariant sheaves on Ĝ with the adjoint action, which by the geometric Satake equivalence matches Z(D‑mod(BunG)). The work of Ben‑Zvi–Francis–Nadler on integral transforms for Drinfeld centers shows that the geometric Langlands equivalence can be reconstructed from the statement that the Drinfeld center of the Hecke category is the category of spectral data. Moreover, the categorical trace of the Hecke category, which computes the Hochschild homology of D‑mod(BunG), is identified with the derived loop space of LocĜ. This identification underlies the geometric trace formula and the spectral decomposition of the categorical Hecke action.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Duality for Parabolic Subgroups

Let P ⊂ G be a parabolic subgroup with Levi factor L. The moduli stack BunP of P-bundles maps to BunG by induction and to BunL by restriction of the Levi quotient. The geometric Langlands correspondence for P predicts a compatibility between the correspondences for G and for L. On the spectral side, the Langlands dual parabolic P∨ ⊂ Ĝ has Levi L∨. The induction functor IndP∨Ĝ on the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on LocĜ corresponds under the geometric Langlands equivalence to the functor EisP of geometric Eisenstein series on D‑mod(BunG). The constant term functor CTP corresponds to the restriction ResP∨Ĝ. The compatibility of these functors with the Hecke actions is expressed by the statement that the diagram of categories and functors commutes up to canonical isomorphism. This parabolic duality is essential for the proof of the categorical geometric Langlands conjecture by induction on the rank of the group: one reduces the statement for G to the statement for its Levi subgroups, using the fact that the category D‑mod(BunG) is generated by Eisenstein series from proper parabolics together with the cuspidal part. The cuspidal part is characterized as the orthogonal complement of the images of all Eisenstein functors, and it corresponds to the “cuspidal” part of IndCoh(LocĜ), i.e. the subcategory orthogonal to all induced objects. The work of Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum on the “cuspidality” condition and of Drinfeld–Lafforgue on the automorphic to spectral direction for GLn provides the foundational results for this parabolic induction/restriction formalism.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Categorical Characters for Loop Groups

The loop group LG = G((t)) acts on the affine Grassmannian GrG. The category of categorical representations of LG (in the sense of Gaitsgory–Lurie) is equivalent to the category of D‑modules on GrG equipped with a compatible action of the positive loop group G[[t]]. The geometric Satake equivalence identifies the spherical subcategory (the G[[t]]-equivariant part) with Rep(Ĝ). The full categorical representation category is the Drinfeld center of the spherical category, and it carries a natural action of the affine Brauer group. The categorical character of a representation is the image of the unit object under the trace map to the Hochschild homology of the category. For LG, the categorical character computes the “character sheaf” on the moduli stack of G-local systems on the punctured disk, which is the local component of the global spectral side. The work of Bezrukavnikov–Finkelberg–Mirković on the categorical characters of affine Lie algebras at critical level shows that these characters are governed by the Feigin–Frenkel center and the algebra of opers. In the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence, the categorical characters of the quantum loop group Uq(L𝔤) at a root of unity give rise to the modular tensor category of representations of the quantum group, which is the category of conformal blocks of the associated WZW model. The Verlinde formula for the fusion rules is recovered as the categorical trace of the tensor product in this modular tensor category.

  • Confusing the categorical Hecke action with the classical Hecke correspondence: the categorical version is a monoidal functor from Rep(Ĝ) to endofunctors of D‑mod(BunG), not merely a correspondence of stacks.
  • Assuming wild ramification only adds a finite‑dimensional Stokes datum: the wild character variety is an infinite‑type stack, and the categorical equivalence requires the full derived enhancement.
  • Treating the Drinfeld center of D‑mod(BunG) as equivalent to Rep(Ĝ) instead of IndCoh(LocĜ).
  • Neglecting the role of the constant term functor in the parabolic induction/restriction formalism: it is the categorical adjoint of Eisenstein series and corresponds to restriction on the spectral side.
  • Identifying categorical characters of the loop group with ordinary characters of the affine Lie algebra: categorical characters live in Hochschild homology and encode the full modular tensor category at critical level.
  • Overlooking the necessity of the derived loop space of LocĜ for the geometric trace formula: the trace of the Hecke category is not the ordinary loop space but its derived enhancement.
  • Assuming the geometric Langlands equivalence for parabolic subgroups follows formally from the equivalence for the Levi: one must also verify compatibility of Hecke actions and the cuspidality condition.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with Fixed Determinant and the Prym Variety

Fix a smooth projective curve X over an algebraically closed field and a reductive group G. The moduli stack BunG of G‑bundles on X decomposes according to the topological type of the bundle, which for a simply connected group is trivial, but for a general reductive group is measured by the fundamental group π1(G). When G = GLn, the determinant map det: BunGLn → BunGL1 ≃ PicX is a morphism of stacks whose fibers are the moduli stacks BunGLnd of bundles with fixed determinant of degree d. The geometric Langlands correspondence for GLn respects this decomposition: the Hecke eigensheaf condition on BunGLnd corresponds to a local system on X with fixed determinant of degree d. The spectral side is the moduli stack LocGLn of rank n local systems, which also fibers over PicX via the determinant. The correspondence induces an equivalence between the derived category of D‑modules on BunGLnd and the derived category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the corresponding component of LocGLn.

For G = SLn, the moduli stack BunSLn is the fiber of the determinant map over the trivial line bundle. The Langlands dual group is PGLn, and the spectral side is LocPGLn. The Prym variety enters when one considers a finite étale cover π: Y → X of degree n. The norm map Nm: PicY → PicX has kernel the Prym variety Prym(Y/X), which is an abelian variety of dimension (n−1)(g−1). The geometric Langlands correspondence for SLn on X is closely related to the correspondence for GLn on Y via the pushforward π* and pullback π* functors. In particular, the Hecke eigensheaves on BunSLn correspond to local systems on X with structure group PGLn, which can be lifted to GLn local systems on Y with trivial determinant. The Prym variety parametrizes the possible lifts, and the categorical equivalence respects this parametrization. The work of Donagi–Pantev and of Arinkin on the geometric Langlands correspondence for abelian varieties shows that the Fourier–Mukai transform on the Prym variety is the geometric Langlands functor for the dual abelian variety, providing a concrete realization of the correspondence in this setting.

The fixed determinant condition also affects the categorical structure. The category D‑mod(BunGLnd) carries an action of the spherical Hecke category Rep(GLn), but the central character of this action is constrained by the determinant. On the spectral side, the category IndCoh(LocGLnd) carries an action of the same Hecke category via convolution with the universal local system. The equivalence intertwines these actions and matches the central characters. For G = Sp2n or SOn, the determinant is replaced by the spinor norm or the Clifford invariant, and the corresponding Prym‑type varieties are the moduli spaces of orthogonal or symplectic bundles with fixed topological invariants. The geometric Langlands correspondence for these groups involves the dual groups SO2n+1 and Sp2n, and the fixed determinant condition translates to a condition on the Stiefel‑Whitney classes of the corresponding local systems. The categorical equivalence is constructed using the geometric Satake equivalence for the dual group and the theory of perverse sheaves on the affine Grassmannian, with the fixed determinant condition implemented by restricting to the appropriate connected component of the affine Grassmannian.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence for GL(2) and the Modular Curve: Explicit Realization via Hecke Operators

For G = GL2 over a curve X of genus g ≥ 2, the moduli stack BunGL2 admits a concrete description in terms of rank 2 vector bundles. The Harder–Narasimhan stratification divides BunGL2 into the open substack of stable bundles and the closed substack of unstable bundles, which is a union of strata indexed by the degree of the maximal destabilizing line subbundle. The geometric Langlands correspondence for GL2 was first established by Lafforgue for function fields and later geometrized by Drinfeld, Laumon, and others. The Hecke operators Hx at a point x ∈ X act on D‑mod(BunGL2) by modifying the bundle at x. A Hecke eigensheaf is a D‑module F equipped with isomorphisms Hx(F) ≃ Vx ⊠ F where Vx is the fiber of a rank 2 local system V at x. The correspondence assigns to each irreducible rank 2 local system V on X a Hecke eigensheaf FV on BunGL2, unique up to isomorphism.

When X is a modular curve Y0(N) over a finite field, the geometric Langlands correspondence for GL2 is intimately connected with the classical modular forms. The moduli stack BunGL2 over Y0(N) parametrizes rank 2 vector bundles with a level N structure. The Hecke operators Tp for primes p ∤ N act on the space of automorphic forms, which are sections of certain line bundles on BunGL2. The geometric Langlands correspondence identifies the Hecke eigenvalues of a cuspidal automorphic form with the Frobenius eigenvalues of the associated Galois representation. In the geometric setting, the D‑module FV corresponding to a local system V is constructed as the intermediate extension of a rank 1 local system on the open stratum of stable bundles, twisted by a certain character of the fundamental group. The singular support of FV is the global nilpotent cone, and its restriction to the unstable strata is described by the geometric Casselman–Shalika formula, which expresses the Whittaker coefficients in terms of the local system V.

The modular curve Y0(N) also carries a family of elliptic curves, and the geometric Langlands correspondence for GL2 on Y0(N) is related to the geometric Langlands correspondence for the elliptic curve via the non‑abelian Hodge correspondence. The Hitchin fibration for GL2 on Y0(N) has base the space of quadratic differentials, and the generic fiber is an abelian variety isogenous to the Jacobian of the spectral curve. The Hecke eigensheaf FV is supported on the nilpotent cone, which is the union of the singular fibers of the Hitchin fibration. The categorical geometric Langlands equivalence for GL2 identifies D‑mod(BunGL2) with IndCoh(LocGL2), and the Hecke action corresponds to the convolution action of the spherical Hecke category. The explicit computation of the Hecke eigenvalues for the Eisenstein series and the cuspidal part reveals the structure of the spectral decomposition: the Eisenstein part corresponds to the induced representations, while the cuspidal part corresponds to the irreducible local systems. The work of Frenkel–Gaitsgory–Vilonen on the geometric Langlands correspondence for GL2 provides a complete description of the categorical equivalence in terms of the geometry of the moduli stack and the representation theory of the affine Lie algebra ŝl2 at the critical level.

Worked Example: Hecke Eigensheaf for GL(2) on P¹
Let X = P¹ and consider the trivial rank 2 local system V = OX ⊕ OX. Describe the Hecke eigensheaf FV on BunGL2 corresponding to V under the geometric Langlands correspondence. Compute the action of the Hecke operator Hx at a point x ∈ X on FV and verify the eigensheaf property.
On P¹, every rank 2 vector bundle splits as O(a) ⊕ O(b) with a ≥ b. The moduli stack BunGL2 is stratified by the splitting type. The trivial local system V = O ⊕ O corresponds to the constant D‑module on the spectral side. Under the geometric Langlands correspondence, the Hecke eigensheaf FV is the constant D‑module on BunGL2 (the structure sheaf OBun viewed as a D‑module). The Hecke operator Hx acts by the correspondence BunGL2 ← Heckex → BunGL2 where Heckex parametrizes modifications of bundles at x. For the constant D‑module, the pull‑push along this correspondence yields Hx(OBun) = (Vx ⊗ OBun) ⊠ OBun where Vx = C² is the fiber of V at x. Since V is trivial, Vx ≃ C² with trivial monodromy, so Hx(FV) ≃ C² ⊠ FV, verifying the eigensheaf property with eigenvalue the standard representation of GL2. The singular support of FV is the zero section of T*BunGL2, which is the global nilpotent cone for the trivial local system.
Exam Strategy: Recognizing the GL(2) Specialization
When a question asks for an explicit geometric Langlands computation, it almost always reduces to GL(2) on P¹ or an elliptic curve. Memorize the stratification of BunGL2 by splitting type, the form of the Hecke correspondence, and the fact that the trivial local system corresponds to the constant D‑module. The eigensheaf property for the trivial local system is a standard check: Hx(O) = (std ⊠ O) where std is the standard representation of GL2. For non‑trivial local systems, the eigensheaf is an intermediate extension from the stable locus; its restriction to unstable strata is governed by the geometric Casselman–Shalika formula. Be ready to write the Hecke correspondence diagram and explain why the constant D‑module is an eigensheaf for the trivial local system.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence for Parabolic Bundles: Induction, Restriction, and the Levi Subgroup

Let G be a reductive group and P ⊂ G a parabolic subgroup with Levi factor L. The moduli stack BunP of P‑bundles on X fits into a diagram BunG ← BunP → BunL where the left arrow is induction of the structure group and the right arrow is restriction to the Levi quotient. On the spectral side, the Langlands dual parabolic P∨ ⊂ Ĝ has Levi L∨, and there is a corresponding diagram LocĜ ← LocP∨ → LocL∨. The geometric Langlands correspondence for P predicts a compatibility between the correspondences for G and L expressed by the commutativity of the diagram of categories and functors up to canonical isomorphism. The induction functor IndP∨Ĝ on IndCoh(LocĜ) corresponds under the geometric Langlands equivalence to the geometric Eisenstein series functor EisP on D‑mod(BunG). The constant term functor CTP corresponds to the restriction ResP∨Ĝ. These functors are adjoint: EisP is left adjoint to CTP, and IndP∨Ĝ is left adjoint to ResP∨Ĝ. The compatibility of the Hecke actions means that the diagram of monoidal functors from the spherical Hecke category Rep(Ĝ) to the endofunctors of the respective categories commutes.

The cuspidal part of D‑mod(BunG) is defined as the orthogonal complement of the images of all Eisenstein functors EisP for proper parabolics P. Under the geometric Langlands equivalence, the cuspidal part corresponds to the “cuspidal” part of IndCoh(LocĜ), i.e., the subcategory orthogonal to all induced objects IndP∨Ĝ. The work of Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum on the cuspidality condition and of Drinfeld–Lafforgue on the automorphic to spectral direction for GLn provides the foundational results for this parabolic induction/restriction formalism. For GLn, the parabolic subgroups correspond to block upper triangular matrices, and the Levi subgroups are products of GLni. The geometric Eisenstein series functor constructs automorphic D‑modules on BunGLn from automorphic D‑modules on BunGLn1 × ⋯ × BunGLnk. The constant term functor computes the constant term of an automorphic D‑module along the unipotent radical of the parabolic. The spectral decomposition of D‑mod(BunGLn) into cuspidal and Eisenstein parts mirrors the Langlands decomposition of the space of automorphic forms.

The parabolic induction/restriction formalism is essential for the proof of the categorical geometric Langlands conjecture by induction on the rank of the group. One reduces the statement for G to the statement for its Levi subgroups, using the fact that the category D‑mod(BunG) is generated by Eisenstein series from proper parabolics together with the cuspidal part. The base case of the induction is the torus, where the geometric Langlands correspondence is the Fourier–Mukai transform on the Picard stack. The compatibility of the Hecke actions with parabolic induction is expressed by the statement that the Hecke functors on D‑mod(BunG) commute with the Eisenstein functors up to a canonical isomorphism involving the Hecke functors on the Levi side. This compatibility is a consequence of the geometric Satake equivalence and the fact that the induction functor IndP∨Ĝ on Rep(Ĝ) corresponds to the tensor product with the representation IndP∨Ĝ(triv). The categorical trace of the Hecke category, which computes the geometric trace formula, also respects the parabolic induction/restriction formalism, and the Arthur–SL2 action on the spectral side intertwines with the parabolic induction on the automorphic side.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence with Framing and the Geometric Satake Equivalence

A framing of a G‑bundle on X at a point x ∈ X is a trivialization of the fiber at x. The moduli stack BunGfr of framed G‑bundles is a G‑torsor over BunG via the action of G on the framing. The geometric Langlands correspondence for framed bundles relates D‑mod(BunGfr) to a category of sheaves on the moduli stack LocĜ with a compatible framing condition. The geometric Satake equivalence identifies the spherical Hecke category Rep(Ĝ) with the category of G[[t]]‑equivariant perverse sheaves on the affine Grassmannian GrG. When a framing is introduced, the affine Grassmannian is replaced by the affine flag variety FlG, and the spherical Hecke category is replaced by the Iwahori–Hecke category, which is the category of I‑equivariant perverse sheaves on FlG where I ⊂ G[[t]] is the Iwahori subgroup. The geometric Satake equivalence for the affine flag variety identifies the Iwahori–Hecke category with the category of representations of the affine Hecke algebra, which is a deformation of the group algebra of the affine Weyl group.

The moduli stack BunGfr carries an action of the loop group LG at the framing point, and the category D‑mod(BunGfr) is a module category over the categorical loop group. The geometric Langlands correspondence for framed bundles identifies D‑mod(BunGfr) with the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of framed local systems LocĜfr. The framing condition on the spectral side is a trivialization of the fiber of the local system at x, which is a Ĝ‑torsor over LocĜ. The Hecke operators at points other than x act on both sides, and at the framing point x the Hecke action is replaced by the action of the loop group. The categorical geometric Satake equivalence for the affine flag variety provides the local model for this correspondence: the category of I‑equivariant D‑modules on FlG is equivalent to the category of modules over the affine Hecke algebra, and this equivalence intertwines the convolution action of the spherical Hecke category with the tensor product action on representations.

The framing also plays a crucial role in the construction of the geometric Langlands functor via the Fourier–Mukai transform. The moduli stack BunGfr is a symplectic stack with a Hamiltonian G‑action, and the Hitchin fibration for framed bundles has a Lagrangian section given by the framing. The geometric Langlands functor is constructed as the Fourier–Mukai transform with kernel the universal Hecke eigensheaf on BunGfr × LocĜfr. The framing trivializes the monodromy at x, which allows one to define the Hecke eigensheaf property without monodromy ambiguity. In the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence, the framing corresponds to a boundary condition in the four‑dimensional N=4 super Yang–Mills theory, and the S‑duality transformation exchanges the framing with a dual framing on the spectral side. The work of Bezrukavnikov–Finkelberg on the geometric Satake equivalence for the affine flag variety and of Gaitsgory on the categorical geometric Langlands correspondence with framing provides the rigorous foundation for this aspect of the program.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence with Level Structure and the Loop Group

A level structure on a G‑bundle at a point x ∈ X is a reduction of the structure group to a congruence subgroup K ⊂ G(Ox) of the formal loop group. The moduli stack BunGK of G‑bundles with level K structure is a finite cover of BunG when K is a principal congruence subgroup. The geometric Langlands correspondence with level structure relates D‑mod(BunGK) to a category of sheaves on the moduli stack LocĜ with a compatible level structure on the local system. The loop group LG = G((t)) acts on the affine Grassmannian GrG, and the level structure corresponds to considering the quotient GrG/K. The geometric Satake equivalence for the congruence subgroup identifies the category of K‑equivariant perverse sheaves on GrG with the category of representations of the quantum group Uq(Ĝ) at a root of unity, where the level of the congruence subgroup determines the root of unity.

The categorical geometric Langlands correspondence with level structure is an equivalence between the derived category of D‑modules on BunGK and the derived category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of Ĝ‑local systems with level structure. The Hecke operators away from x act on both sides, and at x the Hecke action is replaced by the action of the quantum group.
