The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at a root of unity is precisely the geometric Langlands correspondence with level structure, where the parameter q is related to the level by q = exp(2πi/(k + h∨)) for a principal congruence subgroup of level k. The Verlinde formula for the fusion rules of the quantum group is recovered as the categorical trace of the tensor product in the modular tensor category of representations of Uq(Ĝ). The work of Finkelberg–Mirković on the geometric Satake equivalence for quantum groups and of Bezrukavnikov–Finkelberg on the categorical characters of the quantum loop group provides the representation‑theoretic foundation for this correspondence.

The level structure also appears in the physical realization via the Kapustin–Witten equations. A level K structure corresponds to a boundary condition in the four‑dimensional N=4 super Yang–Mills theory that preserves a subgroup of the supersymmetry. The S‑duality transformation exchanges the level structure on the automorphic side with a dual level structure on the spectral side, where the dual group is the Langlands dual Ĝ and the dual level is determined by the Langlands duality of the congruence subgroups. The categorical trace of the Hecke category with level structure computes the geometric trace formula with level, which is the geometric analogue of the Arthur–Selberg trace formula for congruence subgroups. The geometric Langlands correspondence with level structure is essential for the arithmetic applications, as it provides the geometric input for the Langlands correspondence over local and global fields via the function–sheaf dictionary. The work of Gaitsgory–Lurie on the categorical geometric Langlands correspondence with level structure and of Zhu on the geometric Satake equivalence for parahoric subgroups (which are the analogues of congruence subgroups in the affine setting) establishes the rigorous framework for this aspect of the program.

  • Confusing the fixed determinant condition with the trivial determinant condition: for GLn the determinant map has fibers of all degrees, while for SLn the determinant is fixed to be trivial, and the spectral side changes from LocGLn to LocPGLn.
  • Assuming the Hecke eigensheaf for the trivial local system on P¹ is the constant sheaf on the whole BunGL2: it is the constant D‑module, but its singular support is the zero section, not the whole nilpotent cone.
  • Treating parabolic induction as a formal consequence of the equivalence for the Levi: one must verify compatibility of Hecke actions and the cuspidality condition, which is the core of the Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum proof strategy.
  • Identifying the framing moduli stack BunGfr with the trivial G‑torsor over BunG: it is a G‑torsor, but the geometric Langlands functor uses the symplectic structure and the Hamiltonian G‑action, not just the torsor structure.
  • Confusing the level structure on the automorphic side with the level structure on the spectral side: they are Langlands dual, so a principal congruence subgroup of level k for G corresponds to a dual congruence subgroup for Ĝ determined by the dual root system.
  • Assuming the quantum geometric Langlands parameter q is arbitrary: for the correspondence with level structure, q is a root of unity determined by the level and the dual Coxeter number, q = exp(2πi/(k + h∨)).
  • Overlooking the role of the constant term functor in the parabolic induction/restriction formalism: it is the categorical adjoint of Eisenstein series and corresponds to restriction on the spectral side, not merely a pullback.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with Fixed Determinant and the Prym Variety

The geometric Langlands correspondence for a reductive group G on a smooth projective curve X over an algebraically closed field k is an equivalence between the derived category of D‑modules on the moduli stack BunG of G‑bundles on X and the derived category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the moduli stack LocĜ of Ĝ‑local systems on X, where Ĝ is the Langlands dual group. When G = GLn, the determinant map det: BunGLn → Picd(X) induces a decomposition of BunGLn into connected components indexed by the degree d. The geometric Langlands functor respects this decomposition: the component of degree d corresponds to local systems whose determinant has degree d. For SLn, the determinant is fixed to be trivial, so BunSLn is the fiber of det over the trivial line bundle. The spectral side becomes LocPGLn, the moduli of PGLn‑local systems. The Prym variety arises when one considers the correspondence for a double cover π: Y → X. The moduli stack BunSL2(X) of SL2‑bundles on X is related to the moduli stack BunGL1(Y) of line bundles on Y via the norm map Nm: Pic(Y) → Pic(X). The Prym variety Prym(Y/X) is the kernel of Nm on the degree‑zero part. The geometric Langlands correspondence for SL2 on X intertwines the Hecke operators on BunSL2(X) with the action of the dual group PGL2 on LocPGL2(X). The fixed determinant condition forces the spectral local systems to have trivial determinant, which corresponds to the condition that the associated rank‑2 vector bundle has trivial determinant. The Prym variety appears as the fiber of the Hitchin fibration for SL2 over a generic point in the Hitchin base. The work of Donagi–Pantev and of Arinkin–Gaitsgory on the geometric Langlands correspondence for the Prym variety shows that the Fourier–Mukai transform with kernel the Poincaré line bundle on the product of the Prym variety with its dual realizes the correspondence for the abelian part. For non‑abelian groups, the fixed determinant condition modifies the Hecke operators: the Hecke correspondence at a point x ∈ X for SLn is the sub‑correspondence of the GLn Hecke correspondence where the determinant of the modification is trivial. This imposes a constraint on the spectral side: the local system must have trivial determinant, which is the condition that it factors through PGLn. The categorical geometric Langlands correspondence with fixed determinant is an equivalence between D‑mod(BunSLn) and IndCoh(LocPGLn). The constant D‑module on BunSLn corresponds to the structure sheaf of the trivial local system in LocPGLn. The singular support of this D‑module is the global nilpotent cone in T*BunSLn, which is the preimage of the nilpotent cone in T*BunGLn under the projection. The Prym variety enters the picture when one considers the geometric Langlands correspondence for a curve with a marked point and a parabolic structure. The parabolic SLn‑bundles with fixed determinant correspond to local systems with prescribed monodromy at the marked point. The work of Yun on the geometric Langlands correspondence for the Prym variety and of Chen–Zhu on the non‑abelian Hodge correspondence for parabolic bundles provides the rigorous framework for this aspect of the program.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with Level Structure and the Loop Group

The geometric Langlands correspondence with level structure is a refinement of the classical geometric Langlands correspondence that incorporates a congruence subgroup K ⊂ G(𝒪x) at a point x ∈ X. The moduli stack BunG,K of G‑bundles with level K structure classifies G‑bundles on X together with a trivialization of the bundle on the formal disk Dx modulo the action of K. The loop group LG = G((t)) acts on the affine Grassmannian GrG = LG/G(𝒪), and the level structure corresponds to considering the quotient GrG/K. The geometric Satake equivalence for the congruence subgroup identifies the category of K‑equivariant perverse sheaves on GrG with the category of representations of the quantum group Uq(Ĝ) at a root of unity, where the level of the congruence subgroup determines the root of unity. The categorical geometric Langlands correspondence with level structure is an equivalence between the derived category of D‑modules on BunG,K and the derived category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of Ĝ‑local systems with level structure. The Hecke operators away from x act on both sides, and at x the Hecke action is replaced by the action of the quantum group. The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at a root of unity is precisely the geometric Langlands correspondence with level structure, where the parameter q is related to the level by q = exp(2πi/(k + h∨)) for a principal congruence subgroup of level k. The Verlinde formula for the fusion rules of the quantum group is recovered as the categorical trace of the tensor product in the modular tensor category of representations of Uq(Ĝ). The work of Finkelberg–Mirković on the geometric Satake equivalence for quantum groups and of Bezrukavnikov–Finkelberg on the categorical characters of the quantum loop group provides the representation‑theoretic foundation for this correspondence. The level structure also appears in the physical realization via the Kapustin–Witten equations. A level K structure corresponds to a boundary condition in the four‑dimensional N=4 super Yang–Mills theory that preserves a subgroup of the supersymmetry. The S‑duality transformation exchanges the level structure on the automorphic side with a dual level structure on the spectral side, where the dual group is the Langlands dual Ĝ and the dual level is determined by the Langlands duality of the congruence subgroups. The categorical trace of the Hecke category with level structure computes the geometric trace formula with level, which is the geometric analogue of the Arthur–Selberg trace formula for congruence subgroups. The geometric Langlands correspondence with level structure is essential for the arithmetic applications, as it provides the geometric input for the Langlands correspondence over local and global fields via the function–sheaf dictionary. The work of Gaitsgory–Lurie on the categorical geometric Langlands correspondence with level structure and of Zhu on the geometric Satake equivalence for parahoric subgroups (which are the analogues of congruence subgroups in the affine setting) establishes the rigorous framework for this aspect of the program.

Worked Example: Level Structure and Quantum Parameter
Let G = SL2 and let K be the principal congruence subgroup of level k = 1 in SL2(𝒪x). Compute the quantum parameter q for the corresponding quantum geometric Langlands correspondence and identify the quantum group whose representation category appears in the geometric Satake equivalence for K.
For G = SL2, the dual Coxeter number is h∨ = 2. The principal congruence subgroup of level k = 1 corresponds to the quantum parameter q = exp(2πi/(k + h∨)) = exp(2πi/3), a primitive third root of unity. The Langlands dual group of SL2 is PGL2 (or equivalently SO3). The geometric Satake equivalence for the congruence subgroup K identifies the category of K‑equivariant perverse sheaves on the affine Grassmannian GrSL2 with the category of representations of the quantum group Uq(𝔰𝔩2) at q = exp(2πi/3). This quantum group is the Lusztig quantum group at a root of unity, whose representation category is a modular tensor category with three simple objects corresponding to the integrable highest weight modules of level 1 for the affine Lie algebra ŝ𝔩2. The Verlinde formula gives the fusion rules: the tensor product of the two non‑trivial simple objects decomposes as the sum of the trivial object and the other non‑trivial object, reflecting the fusion rules of the SU(2)1 Wess–Zumino–Witten model.
Exam Strategy: Level Structure and Quantum Groups
When a question asks about the geometric Langlands correspondence with level structure, immediately identify the dual Coxeter number h∨ of the group G and the level k of the congruence subgroup. The quantum parameter is q = exp(2πi/(k + h∨)). Remember that the dual group Ĝ appears on the spectral side, and the quantum group is Uq(Ĝ) (or its Langlands dual form). The geometric Satake equivalence for K relates K‑equivariant sheaves on GrG to Rep(Uq(Ĝ)). The Verlinde formula computes the categorical trace of the tensor product in this modular tensor category. Do not confuse the level k on the automorphic side with the level on the spectral side; they are Langlands dual, so the dual level is determined by the dual root system.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with Parahoric Structure

The geometric Langlands correspondence with parahoric structure generalizes the level structure correspondence by allowing the level subgroup K to be a parahoric subgroup of the loop group LG = G((t)) rather than a congruence subgroup. A parahoric subgroup is the stabilizer of a point in the affine Bruhat–Tits building of G over the local field k((t)). The moduli stack BunG,P of G‑bundles with parahoric structure P classifies G‑bundles on X together with a reduction of the structure group to P at the point x. The affine flag variety FlG = LG/I, where I is the Iwahori subgroup, parametrizes the possible reductions. The geometric Satake equivalence for parahoric subgroups, established by Zhu and by Richarz–Zhu, identifies the category of P‑equivariant perverse sheaves on the affine Grassmannian GrG with the category of representations of the quantum group Uq(Ĝ) at a root of unity, where the root of unity is determined by the level of the parahoric subgroup. The categorical geometric Langlands correspondence with parahoric structure is an equivalence between the derived category of D‑modules on BunG,P and the derived category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of Ĝ‑local systems with parahoric structure. The Hecke operators away from x act as usual, while at x the Hecke action is replaced by the action of the quantum group associated to the parahoric subgroup. The parahoric structure on the spectral side corresponds to a parahoric subgroup of the dual loop group LĜ, and the duality of parahoric subgroups under Langlands duality is given by the duality of the corresponding facets in the affine Bruhat–Tits buildings. The work of Gaitsgory–Lurie on the categorical geometric Langlands correspondence with parahoric structure and of Zhu on the geometric Satake equivalence for parahoric subgroups provides the rigorous framework. The parahoric structure is essential for the geometric Langlands correspondence over local fields, as it captures the ramification data of the local Langlands correspondence. The categorical trace of the Hecke category with parahoric structure computes the geometric trace formula with parahoric level, which is the geometric analogue of the Arthur–Selberg trace formula for parahoric subgroups. The geometric Langlands correspondence with parahoric structure also appears in the physical realization via the Kapustin–Witten equations with boundary conditions preserving a parahoric subgroup of the supersymmetry. The S‑duality transformation exchanges the parahoric structure on the automorphic side with the dual parahoric structure on the spectral side.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with Framing and the Geometric Satake Equivalence

The geometric Langlands correspondence with framing is a variant where one considers G‑bundles equipped with a framing at a point x ∈ X, i.e., a trivialization of the fiber of the bundle at x. The moduli stack BunG,fr of framed G‑bundles is a G‑torsor over BunG via the action of G on the framing. The geometric Langlands functor for framed bundles is constructed using the symplectic structure on T*BunG,fr and the Hamiltonian G‑action. The framing moduli stack BunG,fr is isomorphic to the moduli stack of G‑bundles with a section of the associated adjoint bundle at x, which is the same as the moduli stack of G‑bundles with a reduction of the structure group to the trivial group at x. The geometric Satake equivalence for the framed setting identifies the category of G‑equivariant perverse sheaves on the affine Grassmannian GrG with the category of representations of the Langlands dual group Ĝ. The framing introduces a factor of the group G on the automorphic side, which corresponds to the factor of the dual group Ĝ on the spectral side. The categorical geometric Langlands correspondence with framing is an equivalence between the derived category of D‑modules on BunG,fr and the derived category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of framed Ĝ‑local systems LocĜ,fr. The Hecke operators act on both sides, and the framing is preserved by the Hecke correspondences away from x. At the framing point x, the Hecke action is modified by the action of G on the framing. The work of Gaitsgory on the geometric Langlands correspondence with framing and of Braverman–Finkelberg–Nakajima on the geometric Satake equivalence for framed bundles provides the rigorous framework. The framing is also related to the theory of categorical characters: the categorical character of the loop group LG is the category of D‑modules on the moduli stack of framed G‑bundles, and the geometric Langlands correspondence with framing identifies this with the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of framed Ĝ‑local systems. The framing appears in the physical realization via the Kapustin–Witten equations with a Nahm pole boundary condition at x, which corresponds to a framing of the G‑bundle. The S‑duality transformation exchanges the framing on the automorphic side with a dual framing on the spectral side, where the dual group is Ĝ and the dual framing is a trivialization of the Ĝ‑bundle at x. The categorical trace of the Hecke category with framing computes the geometric trace formula with framing, which is the geometric analogue of the Arthur–Selberg trace formula for the group G with a fixed maximal torus.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with Reductions to Levi Subgroups

The geometric Langlands correspondence for reductions to Levi subgroups is a fundamental tool for proving the categorical geometric Langlands correspondence by induction on the semisimple rank of G. Let L ⊂ G be a Levi subgroup of a parabolic subgroup P ⊂ G. The moduli stack BunL of L‑bundles maps to BunG via the induction functor IndPG = q!p*, where p: BunP → BunL is the projection and q: BunP → BunG is the map induced by the inclusion P ⊂ G. The constant term functor CTPG = p!q* is the left adjoint of IndPG. On the spectral side, the dual Levi subgroup L̂ ⊂ Ĝ corresponds to a parabolic subgroup P̂ ⊂ Ĝ, and the induction and restriction functors are defined analogously. The geometric Langlands correspondence is compatible with parabolic induction and restriction: the diagram involving IndPG, CTPG on the automorphic side and the corresponding functors on the spectral side commutes up to natural isomorphism. This compatibility is the core of the Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum proof strategy for the categorical geometric Langlands correspondence. One must verify that the Hecke actions on both sides are compatible with induction and restriction, and that the cuspidality condition (orthogonality to the images of Eisenstein series from proper parabolic subgroups) is preserved. The geometric Eisenstein series functor EisPG = q*p! is the right adjoint of CTPG, and its spectral counterpart is the pushforward along the map LocL̂ → LocĜ. The spectral decomposition of the category of D‑modules on BunG is achieved by iterating the constant term functors for all standard parabolic subgroups. The work of Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum on the categorical geometric Langlands correspondence uses this induction/restriction formalism to reduce the proof to the case of a torus, where the correspondence is the Fourier–Mukai transform. The geometric Langlands correspondence for reductions to Levi subgroups also appears in the physical realization via the Kapustin–Witten equations with boundary conditions corresponding to the Levi subgroup. The S‑duality transformation exchanges the Levi subgroup L on the automorphic side with the dual Levi subgroup L̂ on the spectral side. The categorical trace of the Hecke category with Levi reduction computes the geometric trace formula with Levi reduction, which is the geometric analogue of the Arthur–Selberg trace formula for the Levi subgroup.

  • Confusing the fixed determinant condition with the trivial determinant condition: for GLn the determinant map has fibers of all degrees, while for SLn the determinant is fixed to be trivial, and the spectral side changes from LocGLn to LocPGLn.
  • Assuming the Hecke eigensheaf for the trivial local system on ℙ1 is the constant sheaf on the whole BunGL2: it is the constant D‑module, but its singular support is the zero section, not the whole nilpotent cone.
  • Treating parabolic induction as a formal consequence of the equivalence for the Levi: one must verify compatibility of Hecke actions and the cuspidality condition, which is the core of the Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum proof strategy.
  • Identifying the framing moduli stack BunG,fr with the trivial G‑torsor over BunG: it is a G‑torsor, but the geometric Langlands functor uses the symplectic structure and the Hamiltonian G‑action, not just the torsor structure.
  • Confusing the level structure on the automorphic side with the level structure on the spectral side: they are Langlands dual, so a principal congruence subgroup of level k for G corresponds to a dual congruence subgroup for Ĝ determined by the dual root system.
  • Assuming the quantum geometric Langlands parameter q is arbitrary: for the correspondence with level structure, q is a root of unity determined by the level and the dual Coxeter number, q = exp(2πi/(k + h∨)).
  • Overlooking the role of the constant term functor in the parabolic induction/restriction formalism: it is the categorical adjoint of Eisenstein series and corresponds to restriction on the spectral side, not merely a pullback.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Categorical Representations of the Affine Grassmannian

The affine Grassmannian GrG = G((t))/G[[t]] serves as the geometric incarnation of the loop group representations that underpin the geometric Satake equivalence. In the categorical geometric Langlands program, one replaces the derived category of constructible sheaves on GrG by the DG‑category of ind‑coherent sheaves IndCoh(GrG) equipped with the convolution monoidal structure. This monoidal category is equivalent, via the geometric Satake theorem of Ginzburg and Mirković–Vilonen, to the category Rep(Ĝ) of finite‑dimensional representations of the Langlands dual group Ĝ. The equivalence is monoidal and respects the t‑structures, sending the spherical Hecke category to Rep(Ĝ). When one passes to the categorical level, the Hecke functors acting on D‑modules on BunG are obtained by convolution with objects of IndCoh(GrG). The categorical representation theory of the affine Grassmannian thus provides the algebraic engine that translates spectral data (local systems for Ĝ) into automorphic objects (Hecke eigensheaves). Recent work of Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum and of Arinkin–Gaitsgory shows that the categorical trace of this monoidal category computes the geometric trace formula, linking orbital integrals on the automorphic side to stable orbital integrals on the spectral side. The compatibility of the convolution product with the factorization structure of the affine Grassmannian over the Ran space is essential for establishing the full functoriality of the correspondence.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with a Fixed Determinant and the Prym Variety in Positive Characteristic

Fix a smooth projective curve X over an algebraically closed field of characteristic p > 0. The moduli stack BunGdet=ℒ of G‑bundles with a prescribed determinant line bundle ℒ is a connected component of the full moduli stack. Its spectral counterpart is the moduli stack LocĜdet=ℒ̂ of Ĝ‑local systems with fixed determinant, where ℒ̂ is the dual line bundle determined by the Langlands duality of the center of G. In positive characteristic, the Hitchin fibration acquires a Frobenius‑twisted structure: the Hitchin base receives a map from the de Rham moduli space via the Cartier transform. The Prym variety associated to a spectral cover X̃ → X appears as the fiber of the Hitchin map over a regular semisimple point. The geometric Langlands functor in this setting is expected to intertwine the Frobenius action on D‑modules (or on ℓ‑adic sheaves) with the natural Galois action on the spectral side. The work of Frenkel–Gross and of Zhu on the p‑adic geometric Langlands program shows that the categorical equivalence can be realized after passing to the derived category of ℓ‑adic sheaves and using the ℓ‑adic Riemann–Hilbert correspondence. The fixed‑determinant condition forces the spectral side to be a torsor under the dual abelian variety, and the Prym variety provides the geometric arena where the Hecke eigensheaf property can be checked fiberwise. The compatibility of the Hecke operators with the Frobenius morphism is the key technical input that allows one to descend the correspondence from characteristic zero to positive characteristic.

Worked Example: Hecke Eigensheaf on the Prym Fiber
Let G = SL2 and X a genus‑2 curve over Fp. Consider the Hitchin fibration h: BunSL2 → A where A ≅ H0(X, KX⊗2). For a regular semisimple a ∈ A, the spectral curve X̃a is a double cover of X. The Prym variety Pa = Ker(Nm: Jac(X̃a) → Jac(X)) is an abelian surface. Show that the constant D‑module on the fiber h−1(a) is a Hecke eigensheaf with eigenvalue given by the local system on X̃a corresponding to the trivial character of Pa.
The fiber h−1(a) is a torsor under the Prym variety Pa. The constant D‑module on a torsor under an abelian variety is a Hecke eigensheaf because the Hecke operators act by convolution with the spherical Hecke category, which under geometric Satake corresponds to the representation ring of PGL2. The eigenvalue local system is the pushforward of the trivial local system on X̃a under the norm map, which is precisely the trivial local system on X twisted by the character of Pa determined by the point a. Since the character is trivial, the eigenvalue is the trivial rank‑2 local system on X. The Hecke eigensheaf property follows from the fact that the convolution of the constant D‑module with any spherical Hecke sheaf is again the constant D‑module, because the Hecke correspondence preserves the Hitchin fiber and acts trivially on the Prym torsor. This verifies the required eigensheaf condition.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the Hecke eigensheaf on a Hitchin fiber, first identify the Prym variety as the kernel of the norm map. Recall that the constant D‑module on a torsor under an abelian variety is always a Hecke eigensheaf with eigenvalue given by the trivial local system on the base curve. Write the eigenvalue explicitly as the pushforward of the trivial local system on the spectral curve. Mention the role of the geometric Satake equivalence in translating spherical Hecke sheaves to representations of the dual group.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with a Level Structure and the Loop Group at Critical Level

Introducing a level structure of level k on the automorphic side means fixing a trivialization of the G‑bundle along the formal neighbourhood of a divisor D ⊂ X of degree k. The moduli stack BunGlevel k carries an action of the loop group G((t))/Kk, where Kk is the principal congruence subgroup of level k. At the critical level k = −h∨ (the negative dual Coxeter number), the center of the completed universal enveloping algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra at critical level, known as the Feigin–Frenkel center, becomes large and is isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of opers for the Langlands dual group Ĝ. The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at critical level identifies the category of D‑modules on BunGlevel k with the category of quasi‑coherent sheaves on the moduli of Ĝ‑opers with a prescribed singularity at D. The level structure on the spectral side is a reduction of the Ĝ‑local system to a parabolic subgroup determined by the dual root system; the parameter q = exp(2πi/(k+h∨)) becomes a root of unity. The categorical equivalence is constructed by a version of the geometric Satake equivalence for the affine flag variety, where the convolution category of Kk‑equivariant sheaves on the affine flag variety is equivalent to the category of representations of the quantum group Uq(Ĝ) at the root of unity q. This equivalence respects the factorization structure and yields a fully faithful embedding of the Hecke category into the category of D‑modules on the level‑structured moduli stack.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with a Framing and the Geometric Satake Equivalence for Non‑Simply Connected Groups

A framing at a point x ∈ X is a trivialization of the G‑bundle on the formal disc around x. The framed moduli stack BunG,fr is a G‑torsor over BunG and carries a Hamiltonian G‑action with moment map given by the evaluation of the connection at x. For non‑simply connected groups such as PGLn or SO2n+1, the geometric Satake equivalence must be formulated for the affine Grassmannian of the universal cover G̃ and then descended to G. The dual group Ĝ is then the adjoint form of the Langlands dual of G̃. The framed Hecke category is the category of G‑equivariant sheaves on the affine Grassmannian of G̃, and its categorical trace computes the geometric trace formula with framing. The S‑duality transformation exchanges the framing on the automorphic side with a dual framing on the spectral side, which is a trivialization of the Ĝ‑bundle at x. The compatibility of the Hecke actions with the framing is encoded in the fact that the convolution product on the affine Grassmannian is G‑equivariant. The work of Braverman–Finkelberg–Nakajima on the Coulomb branch of 3‑d N=4 theories provides a physical realization: the framed moduli space is the Higgs branch, and the dual framing corresponds to the Coulomb branch of the mirror theory. The categorical equivalence is then the statement that the category of boundary conditions in the A‑twisted theory on the Higgs branch is equivalent to the category of boundary conditions in the B‑twisted theory on the Coulomb branch.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with Wild Ramification and Irregular Singularities via Spectral Networks

Wild ramification corresponds to allowing irregular singularities in the connections on the spectral side. The moduli stack LocGwild of G‑local systems with irregular singularities at a divisor D is stratified by the formal type of the irregularity. The Hitchin fibration extends to a map from the moduli of meromorphic Higgs bundles with poles of prescribed order to an enlarged Hitchin base. Spectral networks, introduced by Gaiotto–Moore–Neitzke, provide a combinatorial tool to compute the Stokes data of the irregular connection. On the automorphic side, the Hecke operators are modified by inserting a wild ramification defect, which is a category of modules over a quantum group at a root of unity determined by the irregularity. The quantum geometric Langlands parameter q is then exp(2πi/(k+h∨)) where k is the level determined by the pole order. The categorical equivalence is expected to intertwine the wild Hecke category with the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the wild spectral stack, and the spectral networks give a concrete description of the functor in terms of wall‑crossing of stability conditions on the Fukaya category of the spectral curve. Recent work of Bussi–Brav–Joyce on shifted symplectic structures shows that the wild Hitchin moduli space carries a (−1)‑shifted symplectic structure, and the geometric Langlands functor is a Lagrangian correspondence in the derived category of sheaves on this shifted symplectic stack.

  • Confusing the categorical representation of the affine Grassmannian with the classical Satake isomorphism: the former is a monoidal DG‑category, the latter is an isomorphism of commutative rings.
  • Assuming the Prym variety is always an abelian variety of the expected dimension; in positive characteristic it can be non‑reduced or have larger p‑rank.
  • Treating the critical level as an arbitrary integer; it is precisely −h∨ and the Feigin–Frenkel center is only large at this level.
  • Identifying the framed moduli stack BunG,fr with the trivial G‑torsor over BunG without using the Hamiltonian G‑action and moment map.
  • Overlooking that the quantum parameter q becomes a root of unity only when a level structure or wild ramification is present; at generic level q is not a root of unity.
  • Confusing the wild Hecke category with the usual spherical Hecke category; the former involves modules over a quantum group at a root of unity, the latter involves representations of the dual group.
  • Assuming spectral networks give the full functor without mentioning the wall‑crossing of stability conditions on the Fukaya category.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with a Level Structure and the Loop Group at Non‑Critical Level

A level structure on a G‑bundle over a smooth projective curve X is a trivialization of the bundle on a formal neighbourhood of a divisor D of degree n. The moduli stack BunG,lev of G‑bundles with level structure carries a natural action of the loop group LG at level k, where k is an integer distinct from the critical level −h∨. At non‑critical level the affine Kac–Moody algebra 𝔤̂k admits a non‑degenerate invariant bilinear form, and the category of positive energy representations of LG at level k is a modular tensor category. The geometric Hecke operators act on D‑modules on BunG,lev by convolution with the spherical Hecke category of the affine Grassmannian GrG. The quantum parameter q = exp(2πi/(k+h∨)) is not a root of unity, so the quantum geometric Langlands functor is expected to be an equivalence between the derived category of D‑modules on BunG,lev twisted by the determinant line bundle at level k and the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the spectral stack LocĜ equipped with a q‑deformed structure sheaf. The work of Gaitsgory and Lurie on the categorical geometric Langlands at arbitrary level shows that the Hecke eigen‑property is encoded by a factorization algebra on the Ran space of X, and the non‑critical level introduces a non‑trivial braiding on the Hecke category coming from the R‑matrix of the quantum group Uq(𝔤). This braiding is absent at critical level, where the Feigin–Frenkel center provides a large commutative algebra of Hecke operators. Consequently, the spectral decomposition at non‑critical level involves a q‑difference version of the Hitchin integrable system, and the eigen‑sheaves are constructed via the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence for the quantum group at generic q.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with a Parabolic Structure and the Levi Subgroup

Parabolic bundles are G‑bundles equipped with a reduction to a parabolic subgroup P ⊂ G at a finite set of marked points {xi} together with a weight λi in the cocharacter lattice of the Levi factor L = P/U. The moduli stack BunG,par of parabolic G‑bundles is a smooth Artin stack of finite type. The geometric Hecke operators at a marked point xi are modified by the parabolic weight: the Hecke correspondence is taken inside the stack of P‑bundles, and the convolution kernel is a perverse sheaf on the affine flag variety FlG equivariant under the Iwahori subgroup I ⊂ LG. The categorical geometric Langlands conjecture predicts an equivalence between the category of D‑modules on BunG,par with prescribed monodromy at the marked points and the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the stack of Ĝ‑local systems with compatible parabolic structures on the Langlands dual side. The Levi subgroup L appears as the centralizer of the parabolic weight in the dual group Ĝ, and the Hecke category for the parabolic setting is the category of I‑equivariant sheaves on FlG which, by the geometric Satake equivalence for the affine flag variety, is equivalent to the category of representations of the quantum group Uq(Ĝ) at a root of unity determined by the level. Induction and restriction functors between parabolic Hecke categories correspond to the geometric analogues of the Bernstein–Zelevinsky functors for p‑adic groups. The compatibility of the Hecke action with the parabolic structure is encoded in the fact that the moment map for the Hamiltonian L‑action on the framed moduli stack is given by the evaluation of the connection at the marked points. This picture has been made rigorous for GLn by the work of Arinkin and Gaitsgory on the categorical geometric Langlands for parabolic bundles.

Technique: Identifying the Correct Hecke Category for Parabolic Bundles
A question asks: "In the geometric Langlands correspondence for parabolic G‑bundles with weight λ at a point x, which category of sheaves on the affine flag variety FlG describes the Hecke operators?"
The Hecke operators are given by convolution with I‑equivariant perverse sheaves on FlG, where I is the Iwahori subgroup of the loop group LG preserving the parabolic reduction at x. By the geometric Satake equivalence for the affine flag variety, this category is equivalent to the category of finite‑dimensional representations of the quantum group Uq(Ĝ) at a root of unity q = exp(2πi/(k+h∨)) where k is the level determined by the parabolic weight λ. The Levi subgroup L = CentĜ(λ) acts on the Hecke category, and the eigen‑sheaves correspond to ind‑coherent sheaves on the stack of Ĝ‑local systems with a reduction to L at x. Therefore the correct answer is: the Iwahori‑equivariant derived category DI(FlG) of constructible sheaves, or equivalently Rep(Uq(Ĝ)) at the appropriate root of unity.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem mentions parabolic weights or level structures, immediately recall the associated subgroup (Iwahori for parabolic, full loop group for level) and the corresponding quantum group parameter. Write down the equivalence: Hecke category ≅ Rep(Uq(Ĝ)) with q determined by the level or weight. This shortcut avoids re‑deriving the Satake equivalence under time pressure.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with a Fixed Determinant and the Prym Variety in Characteristic Zero

Fix a line bundle ℒ on X of degree d. The moduli stack BunG,det=ℒ of G‑bundles with determinant isomorphic to ℒ is a connected component of BunG. When G = SLn, the determinant condition is automatic; for G = GLn it picks out bundles of fixed degree. The Prym variety Prym(X̃/X) associated to a double cover π: X̃ → X appears as the fiber of the norm map Nm: Pic(X̃) → Pic(X) over ℒ. In characteristic zero the Prym variety is an abelian variety of dimension g(X̃) − g(X). The geometric Langlands correspondence for BunG,det=ℒ is expected to be compatible with the classical abelian Langlands duality for the Prym variety: the category of D‑modules on BunG,det=ℒ is equivalent to the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the stack of Ĝ‑local systems with fixed determinant on the dual side. The Hitchin fibration for BunG,det=ℒ has generic fibers that are torsors over the Prym variety of the spectral curve. The spectral curve Σ ⊂ T*X is defined by the characteristic polynomial of the Higgs field; its normalization Σ̃ carries a natural line bundle whose pushforward gives the Higgs bundle. The Prym variety parametrizes line bundles on Σ̃ with trivial norm, and the Hecke operators act by tensor product with the Poincaré bundle on the Prym. The work of Donagi and Pantev on the geometric Langlands for GLn with fixed determinant shows that the Fourier–Mukai transform with kernel the Poincaré bundle on the Prym variety implements the correspondence. In characteristic zero the Prym is smooth and the Fourier–Mukai kernel is a perfect complex, guaranteeing that the functor is an equivalence of derived categories. This provides a concrete geometric realization of the categorical geometric Langlands for GLn with fixed determinant.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with a Framing and the Geometric Satake Equivalence for Simply Connected Groups

A framing at a point x ∈ X is a trivialization of the G‑bundle on the formal disc Dx. For a simply connected group G̃ the framed moduli stack BunG̃,fr is a G̃‑torsor over BunG̃ and carries a Hamiltonian G̃‑action with moment map given by the evaluation of the connection at x. The geometric Satake equivalence for the affine Grassmannian GrG̃ identifies the spherical Hecke category SphG̃ = DG̃[[t]](GrG̃) with the category Rep(Ĝ) of finite‑dimensional representations of the Langlands dual group Ĝ, which is the adjoint form of the dual of G̃. The framed Hecke category is the category of G̃‑equivariant sheaves on GrG̃, and its categorical trace computes the geometric trace formula with framing. The S‑duality transformation exchanges the framing on the automorphic side with a dual framing on the spectral side, which is a trivialization of the Ĝ‑bundle at x. The compatibility of the Hecke actions with the framing is encoded in the fact that the convolution product on GrG̃ is G̃‑equivariant. The physical realization via the Coulomb branch of three‑dimensional N=4 theories of Braverman–Finkelberg–Nakajima identifies the framed moduli space as the Higgs branch and the dual framing as the Coulomb branch of the mirror theory. The categorical equivalence then states that the category of boundary conditions in the A‑twisted theory on the Higgs branch is equivalent to the category of boundary conditions in the B‑twisted theory on the Coulomb branch. For simply connected groups the dual group Ĝ is adjoint, so the framed geometric Satake equivalence is a monoidal equivalence between the G̃‑equivariant derived category on GrG̃ and the category of representations of the adjoint dual group.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with a Tame Ramification and the Wild Ramification Interface

Tame ramification corresponds to allowing regular singularities with prescribed monodromy conjugacy classes at a divisor D ⊂ X. The moduli stack LocG,tame of G‑local systems with tame ramification is a smooth algebraic stack. The Hitchin fibration extends to a map from the moduli of meromorphic Higgs bundles with simple poles at D to an enlarged Hitchin base. On the automorphic side, the Hecke operators are modified by inserting a tame ramification defect, which is a category of modules over the quantum group Uq(Ĝ) at generic q. The wild ramification interface appears when one allows irregular singularities of higher order; the moduli stack LocG,wild is stratified by the formal type of the irregularity. Spectral networks, introduced by Gaiotto–Moore–Neitzke, provide a combinatorial tool to compute the Stokes data of the irregular connection. The quantum geometric Langlands parameter q becomes a root of unity q = exp(2πi/(k+h∨)) where k is determined by the pole order. The wild Hecke category involves modules over the quantum group at this root of unity, and the categorical equivalence is expected to intertwine the wild Hecke category with the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the wild spectral stack. Recent work of Bussi–Brav–Joyce on shifted symplectic structures shows that the wild Hitchin moduli space carries a (−1)‑shifted symplectic structure, and the geometric Langlands functor is a Lagrangian correspondence in the derived category of sheaves on this shifted symplectic stack. The interface between tame and wild ramification is governed by a wall‑crossing formula for stability conditions on the Fukaya category of the spectral curve, which matches the variation of the quantum parameter q across the critical level.

  • Confusing the non‑critical level Hecke category (modular tensor category) with the critical level spherical Hecke category (commutative algebra from the Feigin–Frenkel center).
  • Assuming the parabolic Hecke category is the same as the spherical Hecke category; the former is Iwahori‑equivariant, the latter is G[[t]]‑equivariant.
  • Treating the Prym variety in characteristic zero as always principally polarized; the polarization type depends on the spectral cover.
  • Identifying the framed moduli stack for a simply connected group with the trivial G‑torsor without using the Hamiltonian G‑action and moment map.
  • Overlooking that the quantum parameter q is a root of unity only for wild ramification or level structure; at generic tame ramification q is not a root of unity.
  • Confusing the wild Hecke category (modules over Uq(Ĝ) at a root of unity) with the tame Hecke category (modules at generic q).
  • Assuming spectral networks alone give the full geometric Langlands functor without invoking wall‑crossing of stability conditions on the Fukaya category.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence for Mixed Characteristic Moduli of G‑Bundles with Fixed Determinant and Prym Varieties

When the base curve X is defined over a field of mixed characteristic, the moduli stack BunG of G‑bundles acquires a richer stratification coming from the reduction modulo p of the determinant line bundle. Fixing a determinant of degree d forces the connected components of BunG to be indexed by the class of d in π1(G) and by the Hodge–Tate weights of the associated p‑adic Galois representations. The Prym variety Prym(Σ/X) attached to a spectral cover Σ → X inherits a natural action of the component group of the centralizer of the Langlands dual group Ĝ, and its polarization type is dictated by the ramification data of the cover. In mixed characteristic the Prym variety is no longer an abelian variety over the base field but a p‑divisible group equipped with a Frobenius and a Verschiebung; its Dieudonné module carries a filtered φ‑module structure that matches the Hodge filtration on the de Rham cohomology of the corresponding Higgs bundle. The geometric Langlands functor in this setting is expected to be realized by a Fourier–Mukai transform whose kernel is a perverse sheaf on the derived fiber product BunG ×LocĜ LocĜ, where LocĜ denotes the derived stack of Ĝ‑local systems with fixed determinant. The compatibility with the p‑adic Hodge theory is encoded in the requirement that the functor intertwines the (φ,Γ)‑module structure on the automorphic side with the filtered φ‑module structure on the spectral side. Recent work of Fargues–Scholze on the geometrization of the local Langlands correspondence provides a template: the Hecke operators at a place v act by correspondences on the moduli of G‑bundles on the Fargues–Fontaine curve, and the resulting categorical equivalence respects the fixed determinant condition because the Hecke correspondences preserve the degree of the determinant line bundle. The Prym variety appears as the fiber of the Hitchin map over a regular semisimple point, and its p‑adic uniformization by a Rapoport–Zink space yields a description of the nearby cycles sheaf that matches the spectral side of the correspondence. This mixed‑characteristic picture also clarifies the role of the Arthur SL2 in the geometric trace formula: the SL2‑action on the cohomology of the Prym variety corresponds to the monodromy operator on the (φ,Γ)‑module, and the weight filtration coincides with the perverse filtration on the Hecke eigensheaves.

Quantum K‑Theory and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for Loop Groups at Non‑Critical Level

The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at a non‑critical level k can be reformulated in the language of quantum K‑theory of the affine Grassmannian GrG. The level k determines a deformation parameter q = exp(2πi/(k+h∨)) which is not a root of unity; the spherical Hecke category SphG(q) is then a braided monoidal category equivalent to the category of finite‑dimensional representations of the quantum group Uq(Ĝ). The convolution product on GrG is deformed by the quantum parameter, and the resulting monoidal structure is governed by the R‑matrix of Uq(Ĝ). On the spectral side, the moduli stack LocĜ of Ĝ‑local systems carries a natural quantization given by the quantum connection on the Hitchin base; its fiber over a point of the Hitchin base is the quantum cohomology ring of the corresponding spectral curve, which is isomorphic to the center of Uq(Ĝ) at the given level. The geometric Langlands functor is expected to be a monoidal equivalence between the category of D‑modules on BunG twisted by the level k line bundle and the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on LocĜ equipped with the quantum connection. The quantum K‑theory of GrG enters through the work of Braverman–Finkelberg–Nakajima, who identified the equivariant quantum K‑theory of the affine Grassmannian with the representation ring of the quantum loop group at level k. This identification provides a concrete description of the Hecke eigensheaves: a Hecke eigensheaf with eigenvalue a Ĝ‑local system E corresponds to a point in the quantum K‑theory spectrum whose Chern character reproduces the spectral data of E. The non‑critical level also allows a comparison with the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence for the dual group Ĝ at level k∨ = -h∨ - k, which is the S‑duality transformation on the quantum parameter q q-1. In this framework the Kapustin–Witten equations with a non‑zero theta angle give a physical realization: the A‑twisted theory on the Hitchin moduli space at level k is mirror to the B‑twisted theory on the dual Hitchin moduli space at level k∨. The quantum K‑theory perspective also clarifies the role of the Verlinde algebra: the fusion rules of the modular tensor category SphG(q) are precisely the structure constants of the quantum K‑theory ring of GrG, and the Verlinde formula computes the dimension of the space of conformal blocks as the Euler characteristic of a certain line bundle on the moduli of parabolic bundles.

Worked Example: Computing a Hecke Eigenvalue via Quantum K‑Theory
Let G = SL2 and k = 1 (so q = exp(2πi/3)). The affine Grassmannian GrSL2 has Schubert cells indexed by dominant coweights n ≥ 0. The quantum K‑theory ring QKT(Gr) is generated by the class of the basic representation V1 with relation [V1] ⋆ [V1] = [V2] + q [V0]. A Hecke eigensheaf F on BunSL2 with eigenvalue a rank‑2 local system E corresponds to a point in Spec QKT(Gr) where the Chern character of [V1] equals the trace of the monodromy of E around a chosen loop. Compute the eigenvalue of the Hecke operator H1 acting on F.
The Hecke operator H1 is the convolution with the IC‑sheaf of the Schubert cell Gr1. Under the quantum Satake equivalence this corresponds to the class [V1] in QKT(Gr). The eigenvalue is the value of the Chern character ch([V1]) at the point determined by E. For SL2 the Chern character of [V1] is 2 cosh(λ/2) where λ is the logarithm of the monodromy eigenvalue. At level k = 1 the quantum parameter q = exp(2πi/3) imposes the relation 2 cosh(λ/2) = q + q-1 = -1. Hence the eigenvalue of H1 on F is -1.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the eigenvalue of a Hecke operator at a specific non‑critical level, first identify the quantum parameter q from the level, then write the quantum Satake relation for the fundamental representation. The eigenvalue is the specialization of the Chern character at the point of the quantum K‑theory spectrum corresponding to the given local system. Memorize the basic relation [V1] ⋆ [V1] = [V2] + q [V0] for SL2 and its analogues for higher rank; this avoids re‑deriving the whole quantum K‑theory ring under time pressure.

Wild Ramification, Parabolic Structures, and the Levi Subgroup in the Geometric Langlands Program

Wild ramification corresponds to allowing irregular singularities of order greater than one in the connection on a G‑bundle. The formal type of an irregular singularity at a point x ∈ X is encoded by a leading term in the Laurent expansion of the connection, which takes values in a Levi subalgebra 𝔩 ⊂ 𝔤 after a suitable gauge transformation. The moduli stack LocG,wild of G‑local systems with prescribed wild ramification is stratified by the conjugacy class of this leading term; each stratum is a gerbe over a product of a tame moduli space and a formal moduli space of irregular connections. On the automorphic side, the Hecke category is enlarged to a wild Hecke category whose objects are modules over the quantum group Uq(Ĝ) at a root of unity q = exp(2πi/(k+h∨)) where k is determined by the pole order. The parabolic structure at x is a reduction of the G‑bundle to a parabolic subgroup P with Levi factor L; the corresponding Hecke operators are given by convolution with sheaves on the affine flag variety FlG that are equivariant under the Iwahori subgroup I ⊂ G((t)). The geometric Langlands functor is expected to intertwine the parabolic Hecke category (I‑equivariant sheaves on FlG) with the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the wild spectral stack equipped with a compatible parabolic reduction on the dual side. The Levi subgroup L appears naturally as the centralizer of the leading term of the irregular connection; its Langlands dual ˇL is a Levi subgroup of Ĝ, and the wild Hecke category decomposes as a tensor product of a tame Hecke category for ˇL and a nilpotent Hecke category governed by the nilpotent cone in the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical of the dual parabolic. Spectral networks, introduced by Gaiotto–Moore–Neitzke, provide a combinatorial description of the Stokes data of the irregular connection: the walls of the network correspond to BPS states, and the wall‑crossing formula for the associated stability conditions on the Fukaya category of the spectral curve matches the variation of the quantum parameter q across the critical level. The compatibility of the parabolic and wild structures is encoded in the fact that the moment map for the Hamiltonian action of L on the wild Hitchin moduli space coincides with the evaluation of the connection at x, and the symplectic reduction by L yields the tame Hitchin moduli space for the Levi subgroup. This picture also explains the appearance of the Drinfeld compactification of the group G in the geometric Langlands correspondence with wild ramification: the compactification provides a proper moduli space for the parabolic bundles, and the boundary strata correspond to the different possible leading terms of the irregular connection.

Coulomb Branches, Framings, and the Geometric Satake Equivalence for Non‑Simply Connected Groups

For a non‑simply connected reductive group G̃, the Langlands dual group Ĝ is not simply connected either; its center Z(Ĝ) is isomorphic to the fundamental group π1(G̃). A framing at a point x ∈ X is a trivialization of the G̃‑bundle at x, which is equivalent to a choice of a point in the affine Grassmannian GrG̃ fixed by the loop group G̃[[t]]. The framed moduli stack BunG̃,fr carries a Hamiltonian action of G̃ with moment map given by the evaluation of the connection at x. The geometric Satake equivalence for the affine Grassmannian identifies the spherical Hecke category SphG̃ = DG̃[[t]](GrG̃) with Rep(Ĝ), the category of finite‑dimensional representations of the adjoint form of the dual group. When a framing is present, the relevant Hecke category is the G̃‑equivariant derived category DG̃(GrG̃), which is equivalent to the category of representations of the full dual group Ĝ (including its center). The S‑duality transformation exchanges the framing on the automorphic side with a dual framing on the spectral side, which is a trivialization of the Ĝ‑bundle at x. Physically, this is realized by the Coulomb branch of a three‑dimensional N = 4 supersymmetric gauge theory constructed by Braverman–Finkelberg–Nakajima: the Higgs branch of the theory is the framed moduli space BunG̃,fr, while the Coulomb branch is the dual framed moduli space LocĜ,fr. The categorical equivalence states that the category of boundary conditions in the A‑twisted theory on the Higgs branch is equivalent to the category of boundary conditions in the B‑twisted theory on the Coulomb branch. For simply connected groups the dual group Ĝ is adjoint, so the framed geometric Satake equivalence is a monoidal equivalence between the G̃‑equivariant derived category on GrG̃ and Rep(Ĝ) with the trivial central character. For non‑simply connected groups the center of Ĝ acts non‑trivially, and the equivalence must keep track of the central character, which corresponds to the choice of a component of the framed moduli stack. The compatibility of the Hecke actions with the framing is encoded in the fact that the convolution product on GrG̃ is G̃‑equivariant, and the monoidal structure on DG̃(GrG̃) matches the tensor product of representations of Ĝ. This framework also explains the appearance of the Vinberg monoid in the geometric Langlands correspondence with framing: the Vinberg monoid provides a canonical compactification of the group G̃ that respects the framing, and its intersection cohomology sheaves give the kernels for the Hecke functors on the framed moduli stack.

Derived Stacks, Positive Characteristic, and the Prym Variety in the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

In positive characteristic p the moduli stack BunG of G‑bundles on a smooth projective curve X is an Artin stack of infinite type, but its derived enhancement captures the infinitesimal deformation theory of bundles with fixed determinant. The derived stack BunGder carries a natural (−1)‑shifted symplectic structure coming from the Atiyah class of the universal bundle, and the Hitchin map extends to a morphism of derived stacks h: T*BunGder → AHitchin where AHitchin is the derived Hitchin base. The Prym variety Prym(Σ/X) associated to a spectral cover Σ → X is replaced by the derived Prym stack Prymder(Σ/X), whose tangent complex at a point is the cohomology of the complex of sheaves on Σ computing the deformation theory of line bundles with norm trivial on X. The geometric Langlands functor in positive characteristic is expected to be a Fourier–Mukai transform with kernel given by the derived Poincaré bundle on the derived fiber product BunGder ×LocĜder LocĜder. The Frobenius morphism on X induces a correspondence on BunGder that matches the geometric Frobenius on LocĜder under the Langlands correspondence; this is the geometric analogue of the compatibility of the classical Langlands correspondence with the Frobenius action on Galois representations. The derived structure also resolves the issue of the non‑smoothness of the moduli of bundles with fixed determinant: the classical truncation of BunGder is the usual stack, but the derived structure remembers the obstruction theory, which is essential for defining the perverse sheaves that appear as Hecke eigensheaves. Recent work of Bhatt–Lurie on prismatic cohomology provides a p‑adic analogue of the derived de Rham complex that computes the cohomology of the derived moduli stack, and the resulting prismatic crystals correspond to the ind‑coherent sheaves on the derived spectral stack. The Prym variety in positive characteristic is a p‑divisible group whose Dieudonné module is the prismatic cohomology of the spectral cover; its Newton polygon is determined by the slopes of the Frobenius on the Higgs field, and the geometric Langlands equivalence predicts that the Newton polygon of the Prym variety matches the Hodge polygon of the corresponding Higgs bundle, a statement known as the p‑adic non‑abelian Hodge theorem.

  • Confusing the mixed‑characteristic Prym variety (a p‑divisible group) with the characteristic‑zero Prym variety (an abelian variety).
  • Assuming the quantum parameter q is a root of unity at every non‑critical level; it is a root of unity only when the level is an integer multiple of the dual Coxeter number.
  • Treating the wild Hecke category for a given irregular type as identical to the tame Hecke category for the Levi subgroup; the wild category carries an additional nilpotent monodromy datum.
  • Identifying the framed geometric Satake equivalence for a non‑simply connected group with the spherical equivalence for its adjoint dual; the framing forces the full dual group including its center.
  • Overlooking that the derived enhancement of BunG is necessary to define the (−1)‑shifted symplectic structure used in the Fourier–Mukai kernel.
  • Believing the spectral network wall‑crossing formula applies only to the critical level; it governs the variation of stability conditions for any quantum parameter q.
  • Neglecting the role of the Vinberg monoid in compactifying the framed moduli stack; without it the Hecke functors are not proper.

These advanced topics illustrate how the geometric Langlands program intertwines derived algebraic geometry, quantum groups, and p‑adic Hodge theory to produce a unified duality across characteristics and ramification types.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Categorical Representations of the Double Affine Hecke Algebra

The double affine Hecke algebra (DAHA) of type G was introduced by Cherednik in the 1990s as a deformation of the group algebra of the double affine Weyl group. In the geometric Langlands program the DAHA appears naturally as the endomorphism algebra of the universal Whittaker sheaf on the affine flag variety. Bezrukavnikov and Finkelberg proved that the category of modules over the DAHA at a generic parameter is equivalent to the category of G-equivariant coherent sheaves on the Steinberg variety of the Langlands dual group Ĝ. This equivalence is a categorical incarnation of the classical Fourier transform on the affine Grassmannian and provides a bridge between the representation theory of the DAHA and the geometric Satake correspondence.

At the critical level the DAHA specializes to the spherical Hecke algebra of the loop group, and its categorical representations are governed by the derived category of D-modules on the moduli stack of G-bundles. The work of Varagnolo and Vasserot identifies the center of the DAHA with the algebra of functions on the space of opers for Ĝ, matching the Feigin–Frenkel center in the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence. Consequently, the DAHA furnishes a concrete algebraic model for the quantum parameter q and its specialization at roots of unity, where the representation theory becomes modular and connects to the theory of quantum groups.

Recent advances by Oblomkov and Yun construct a categorical action of the DAHA on the derived category of coherent sheaves on the Hilbert scheme of points on the cotangent bundle of the curve. This action categorifies the polynomial representation of the DAHA and yields a geometric realization of the Macdonald polynomials as characters of certain perverse sheaves. The compatibility of this categorical action with the Hecke functors on the moduli of bundles is a key ingredient in the proof of the categorical geometric Langlands conjecture for GLn at generic level.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Duality for Quantum Groups at Roots of Unity

Quantum groups at roots of unity provide a finite‑dimensional ribbon category that mirrors the modular tensor categories arising from the representation theory of affine Lie algebras at critical level. In the geometric Langlands setting the quantum group Uq(Ĝ) with q a primitive ℓ-th root of unity (where ℓ is coprime to the Coxeter number) controls the monodromy of the quantum connection on the moduli of G-local systems. The work of Bezrukavnikov, Finkelberg, and Ostrik establishes an equivalence between the derived category of representations of the small quantum group and the derived category of coherent sheaves on the nilpotent cone of Ĝ, which is the fiber of the Hitchin map over zero.

This equivalence is compatible with the geometric Langlands functor: the Hecke eigensheaf condition translates into the statement that the corresponding object in the quantum group category is a module over the quantum Frobenius kernel. The quantum parameter q is identified with the exponentiated level exp(2πi/(k+h∨)), so that the critical level corresponds to q=1 and non‑critical levels give genuine roots of unity. The modular structure of the quantum group category yields the Verlinde formula for the dimensions of spaces of conformal blocks, which matches the computation of the categorical trace of the Hecke functors.

At the level of the Hitchin fibration the quantum group duality manifests as a mirror symmetry between the B-model on the moduli of G-Higgs bundles and the A-model on the moduli of Ĝ-local systems with a fixed monodromy at the punctures. The wall‑crossing phenomena for stability conditions on the Fukaya category are governed by the same quantum group representations, providing a physical interpretation of the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence via the Kapustin–Witten topological twist of N=4 super Yang–Mills theory.

Worked Example: Identifying the Quantum Parameter
Let G = SL2 and consider the geometric Langlands correspondence at level k = 1. The dual Coxeter number is h∨ = 2. Compute the quantum parameter q and determine whether the corresponding quantum group Uq(sl2) is at a root of unity.
The quantum parameter is defined by q = exp(2πi/(k+h∨)). Substituting k = 1 and h∨ = 2 gives q = exp(2πi/3), a primitive third root of unity. Since q is a root of unity, the small quantum group Uq(sl2) is finite‑dimensional and its representation category is a modular tensor category. This matches the expectation that level k = 1 for SL2 corresponds to the SU(2) Wess–Zumino–Witten model at level 1, whose conformal blocks are governed by the quantum group at a third root of unity.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the quantum parameter, always write the formula q = exp(2πi/(k+h∨)) and substitute the given level and dual Coxeter number. Check whether the denominator divides the numerator to decide if q is a root of unity; this determines whether the quantum group is semisimple (generic q) or modular (root of unity). Remember that the critical level corresponds to k = -h∨, giving q = 1.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with a Fixed Determinant and the Prym Variety in Mixed Characteristic

In mixed characteristic the moduli stack BunG of G-bundles on a smooth projective curve over a p-adic field carries a natural prismatic structure introduced by Bhatt and Lurie. The derived enhancement BunGder admits a (−1)-shifted symplectic form coming from the Atiyah class of the universal bundle, and the Hitchin map extends to a morphism of derived stacks h: T*BunGder → AHitchin where the base is the derived Hitchin space. The Prym variety associated to a spectral cover Σ → X is replaced by the derived Prym stack Prymder(Σ/X), whose tangent complex computes the deformation theory of line bundles on Σ with norm trivial on X.

The geometric Langlands functor in this setting is expected to be a Fourier–Mukai transform with kernel given by the derived Poincaré bundle on the derived fiber product BunGder ×LocĜ LocĜ. The Frobenius morphism on the curve induces a correspondence on BunGder that matches the geometric Frobenius on LocĜ under the Langlands equivalence; this is the geometric analogue of the compatibility of the classical Langlands correspondence with the action of the absolute Galois group on ℓ‑adic representations.

Prismatic cohomology provides a p-adic analogue of the derived de Rham complex that computes the cohomology of BunGder. The resulting prismatic crystals correspond to ind‑coherent sheaves on the derived spectral stack, and the Newton polygon of the Prym p-divisible group is determined by the slopes of the Frobenius on the Higgs field. The p-adic non‑abelian Hodge theorem predicts that this Newton polygon coincides with the Hodge polygon of the corresponding Higgs bundle, a statement proved in the case G = GLn by Liu and Zhu using the Fargues–Fontaine curve.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for the Moduli of G‑Bundles with a Parabolic Structure and the Levi Subgroup in Positive Characteristic

Parabolic bundles in positive characteristic are defined by a reduction of the structure group to a parabolic subgroup P ⊂ G at a finite set of marked points, together with a filtration of the fiber compatible with the root spaces of P. The moduli stack BunG,P of such bundles carries a natural stratification by the Harder–Narasimhan type of the underlying bundle. In characteristic p the Frobenius morphism pulls back a parabolic bundle to a new parabolic bundle whose weights are multiplied by p; this operation is central to the geometric Langlands correspondence with parabolic structure.

The Hecke functors for parabolic bundles are constructed using the affine flag variety of the Levi factor L = P/U, where U is the unipotent radical. The geometric Satake equivalence for the affine flag variety identifies the category of L-equivariant perverse sheaves on the affine flag variety with the representation category of the Langlands dual Levi Ĥ. This equivalence is compatible with the induction and restriction functors associated to the inclusion Ĥ Ĝ, reflecting the functoriality of the Langlands correspondence under Levi subgroups.

Recent work of Gaitsgory and Raskin shows that the categorical geometric Langlands conjecture for parabolic bundles in positive characteristic follows from the existence of a p-adic interpolation of the Hecke eigensheaf condition. The interpolation uses the theory of (φ,Γ)-modules on the Fargues–Fontaine curve to relate the Frobenius action on the moduli of parabolic bundles to the monodromy of the corresponding local system. The result yields a precise description of the wild ramification data in terms of the nilpotent orbits of the Levi factor, extending the classical Deligne–Langlands classification to the geometric setting.

The Geometric Langlands Correspondence and the Theory of Categorical Representations of the Affine Grassmannian at Critical Level

The affine Grassmannian GrG is the ind‑scheme representing the functor of G-bundles on the formal disk together with a trivialization on the punctured disk. At the critical level the category of D-modules on GrG carries a natural action of the Feigin–Frenkel center, which is isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of Ĝ-opers on the formal disk. The categorical geometric Satake equivalence identifies the spherical Hecke category DG(O)(GrG) with the category of representations of the Langlands dual group Ĝ, and the central character corresponds to the choice of an oper.

Bezrukavnikov and Finkelberg constructed a categorical action of the affine braid group on the derived category of coherent sheaves on the Steinberg variety of Ĝ, which categorifies the action of the affine Hecke algebra on the representation ring of Ĝ. This action lifts to an action of the categorical Hecke operators on the category of D-modules on BunG, and the Hecke eigensheaf condition is precisely the statement that the object is an eigenobject for this categorical action with eigenvalue given by the oper.

At the critical level the quantum parameter q equals 1, and the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence reduces to the classical geometric Langlands correspondence. The categorical trace of the Hecke functors computes the character of the corresponding representation of Ĝ, and the Verlinde formula for the dimensions of spaces of conformal blocks follows from the categorical trace formula. This picture is compatible with the Kapustin–Witten interpretation: the critical level corresponds to the topological B-model on the Hitchin moduli space, where the branes are supported on the fibers of the Hitchin fibration and the category of branes is equivalent to the category of D-modules on BunG.

  • Confusing the double affine Hecke algebra with the ordinary affine Hecke algebra; the DAHA contains two commuting copies of the affine Weyl group and a central parameter q.
  • Assuming the quantum group at a root of unity is semisimple; it is not, and its representation category is a modular tensor category with non‑trivial braiding.
  • Treating the mixed‑characteristic Prym stack as an ordinary abelian variety; it is a derived stack whose truncation is a p-divisible group.
  • Neglecting the effect of Frobenius on parabolic weights in positive characteristic; the weights are multiplied by p under pullback.
  • Identifying the critical level with a generic quantum parameter; the critical level forces q = 1 and the quantum group collapses to the universal enveloping algebra.
  • Overlooking the role of the Feigin–Frenkel center in controlling the central character of Hecke eigensheaves at critical level.
  • Believing the categorical geometric Satake equivalence for the affine flag variety is the same as the spherical equivalence; the former involves the full affine braid group action.

Mixed Characteristic Geometric Langlands and the Prym Variety

The mixed characteristic geometric Langlands program studies the correspondence for a smooth projective curve X over a perfect field k of characteristic p > 0, while allowing the coefficient ring to be a complete discrete valuation ring O of characteristic zero with residue field k. The central object is the moduli stack Bun_G of G‑bundles on X, now considered as a derived stack over O. The Prym variety appears when one fixes a determinant line bundle on a G‑bundle and considers the fiber of the determinant map det: Bun_G → Pic^d(X). For G = GL_n this fiber is a torsor under the Prym variety associated to a spectral cover π: X̃ → X coming from the Hitchin fibration. In mixed characteristic the Prym variety is replaced by a p‑divisible group whose Dieudonné module encodes the Frobenius action on the cohomology of the Hitchin fiber. Recent work of Zhu and of Bhatt–Scholze constructs a categorical equivalence between the derived category of ℓ‑adic sheaves on Bun_G (for ℓ ≠ p) and the derived category of coherent sheaves on the stack of Langlands parameters LocSys_Ĝ, compatible with the Hecke functors. The equivalence is proved by reducing to the case of a constant group scheme over O and using the p‑adic Simpson correspondence of Liu–Zhu to relate Higgs bundles to local systems. The Prym p‑divisible group controls the monodromy of the Hecke eigensheaves along the fibers of the Hitchin map, and its Newton polygon determines the support of the corresponding spectral sheaf. This picture extends the classical geometric Langlands correspondence over ℂ to a setting where both the curve and the coefficients have arithmetic significance.

Critical Level Loop Group Geometric Langlands for Non‑Simply Connected Groups

At the critical level the affine Kac–Moody algebra of a reductive group G has central charge equal to the negative dual Coxeter number. The category of D‑modules on the affine Grassmannian Gr_G carries an action of the Feigin–Frenkel center, which is isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of Ĝ‑opers on the formal disk. When G is not simply connected, the affine Grassmannian decomposes into connected components indexed by the fundamental group π₁(G). The categorical geometric Satake equivalence identifies the spherical Hecke category DG(O)(Gr_G) with the category of representations of the Langlands dual group Ĝ, but the central character now depends on the component of Gr_G. For non‑simply connected G, the Hecke eigensheaf condition must be imposed on each component separately, and the eigenvalue oper acquires a twist by a character of π₁(G). Bezrukavnikov and Finkelberg showed that the categorical action of the affine braid group on the derived category of coherent sheaves on the Steinberg variety of Ĝ lifts to an action on D‑modules on Bun_G, and the Hecke eigensheaves correspond to objects that are eigen for this action with eigenvalue given by the twisted oper. The quantum parameter q equals 1 at critical level, so the quantum group collapses to the universal enveloping algebra, and the correspondence reduces to the classical geometric Langlands correspondence for the loop group LG. The categorical trace of the Hecke functors computes the character of the corresponding representation of Ĝ, and the Verlinde formula for the dimensions of spaces of conformal blocks follows from the categorical trace formula. This framework also accommodates the Kapustin–Witten interpretation: the critical level corresponds to the topological B‑model on the Hitchin moduli space, where branes are supported on the fibers of the Hitchin fibration and the category of branes is equivalent to the category of D‑modules on Bun_G.

Worked Example: Hecke Eigensheaf Condition on a Non‑Simply Connected Group
Let G = PGL₂ over a curve X. The affine Grassmannian GrPGL₂ has two connected components indexed by π₁(PGL₂) ≅ ℤ/2. Describe the Hecke eigensheaf condition for a D‑module F on BunPGL₂ with eigenvalue given by a GL₂‑oper χ on the formal disk, and explain how the component twist appears.
The moduli stack BunPGL₂ classifies rank‑2 vector bundles with trivial determinant up to isomorphism. The Hecke functor H_x at a point x ∈ X acts on D‑modules on BunPGL₂ by modifying the bundle at x. Because GrPGL₂ has two components, the Hecke functor decomposes as H_x = H_x⁰ ⊕ H_x¹, where H_x⁰ preserves the component of the trivial bundle and H_x¹ swaps the two components. A Hecke eigensheaf F must satisfy H_x⁰(F) ≅ χ⁰ ⊠ F and H_x¹(F) ≅ χ¹ ⊠ F, where χ⁰ and χ¹ are the restrictions of the GL₂‑oper χ to the two components of the oper space. The oper χ corresponds to a connection on a rank‑2 bundle on the formal disk; its restriction to the identity component gives the usual eigenvalue, while the non‑trivial component contributes a twist by the non‑trivial character of π₁(PGL₂). Concretely, if χ is represented by a connection ∇ = d + (0 1; t 0) dt/t, then χ⁰ is the oper for SL₂ and χ¹ is the same oper tensored with the sign character of ℤ/2. The eigensheaf condition therefore couples the two components of GrPGL₂ and forces F to be a direct sum of two D‑modules exchanged by H_x¹, each an eigensheaf for H_x⁰ with eigenvalue χ⁰. This matches the categorical geometric Satake prediction that the spherical Hecke category for PGL₂ is the category of representations of SL₂ equipped with a ℤ/2‑grading.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks about the critical level for a non‑simply connected group, first identify the fundamental group π₁(G) and the resulting decomposition of the affine Grassmannian. Then recall that the Feigin–Frenkel center acts through opers twisted by characters of π₁(G). The Hecke eigensheaf condition splits accordingly.
